[trialAccount t18600507-488]
488. MARY ANN LEHO (22), was indicted for feloniously uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. POLAND and ORRIDGE conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE ROGERS ( Policeman Z, 53). I produce a certificate ( Read: "Central Criminal Court, May, 1859, Mary Ann Leho was convicted unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin, and Confined Nine Months. ") The prisoner is the person—I had her in custody.
ALICE CASTLEDINE . My uncle's name is Thorn—he keeps a shop in Gravel-lane, Southwark—on 20th February I was in the shop—the prisoner came in—I served her a half-quarten loaf—she gave me in payment a half-crown—I looked at it, and told her it was bad—she said she was not aware of it—I went in to my uncle and gave him the half-crown—my uncle came into the shop and began to speak to her—she ran out of the shop.
Prisoner. Q. I did not run out of the shop. A. Yes, you did, and my uncle ran after you.
RICHARD THORN . I live at 36, Gravel-lane, and am a baker—on that day I was in the parlour; my niece brought the half-crown to me—I went in the shop to the prisoner, and asked her where she got it—she said she got it in payment for wages from a rope-maker in Holland-street—I said I would send for a constable to take her up—she ran out of the shop, and turned from Gravel-lane to Bear-lane—I followed, and brought her back, and gave her in custody—I gave the half-crown to the policeman.
CHARLES TAYLOR . ( Policeman A, 500). On 20th February the prisoner was given into my custody by the last witness, with this half-crown—she was taken to the police-station, and she gave the name of Mary Drake—she was remanded till the 29th, and discharged.
ANN MARCHANT . I am the wife of John Marchant, a news-agent, 10, Maze-pond—on 20th April the prisoner came to my shop and asked for "Bell's Life"—I told her I had not one, and she said, "I will take a 'London Journal'"—she put down a sixpence—I put it in my mouth, and found it was bad—I said, "O, dear, here is another bad sixpence!"—she ran out of the house—I had the sixpence in my mouth—I ran after her, and caught her by the back of the neck—I held her—she scratched my arm and made it bleed, but I held her till the officer came, and gave her in custody—I gave the officer the sixpence.
HENRY SKINNER . ( Police Sergeant M 7). The prisoner was given into my custody—I received this sixpence from the last witness—the prisoner gave the name of Mary Parker—she had a purse in her hand, which contained one good sixpence.
WILLIAM WEBSTER . These are both bad.
GUILTY .— Four Years' Penal Servitude.
[trialAccount t18600507-489]
489. WILLIAM WRIGHT (41), was indicted for unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. POLAND and ORRIDGE conducted the Prosecution.
ANN LAPPIDGE . I am the wife of James Lappidge, who keeps the King's Head public-house, in Tooley-street—on 20th April I was in the bar between
5 and 6 o'clock—the prisoner came in and asked for half a pint of porter—I served him with it—he gave me a sixpence—I gave him 5 d. change—I put the sixpence in the till—there was no other sixpence there—the prisoner went out, and in three or four minutes he came back, and asked for a pennyworth of gin—no person had come in between the time of the prisoner going out and coming in again—I served him with the gin, and he gave me another sixpence—I looked at it, and bent it, and told him it was bad—he told me it was not—I then looked at the other sixpence that I had put in the till—I broke it in two pieces, and told him I had taken that from him before—I said, "That you just gave me was a bad one"—he said, "That is a mistake, some one else must have given it you, I have never been here before"—I am quite positive he is the man; I had seen him many times before—he left the house; my husband followed him, and gave him in custody—I gave the coins to my husband, and he gave them to the officer.
Prisoner. Q. I never was in your house before—you bit several more sixpences with your teeth. A. No; I never touched another one—I have seen you repeatedly before.
JAMES LAPPIDGE . I am husband of the last witness—on 20th April she gave me a bad sixpence, and two pieces of a sixpence, all bad—I had seen the prisoner come in, and my wife serve him with the beer—I went into the bar-parlour, and my wife called me back—the prisoner had just come in again for some gin—I am quite sure he is the man—I have seen him before—my wife gave me the sixpences, the prisoner left, and I followed him—he joined another man at the corner of Weston-street, and they went into the Hop Pole beer-shop—I spoke to the policeman, and we went in and took him—I gave the sixpences to the constable.
HENRY BISHOP . I am a labourer, and live at No. 4, Chapel-court, Norfolk-street, Southwark—I was in the Hop Pole when the policeman came in—the prisoner was there with another man—the prisoner was sitting in a dark box—he and the other man were sitting there drinking together—I saw the policeman take the prisoner away—I saw where he had been sitting, a parcel containing five sixpences, wrapped in separate papers—I gave them to the constable.
WILLIAM ENDACOTT ( Policeman A, 487). On 20th April Mr. Lappidge spoke to me, and took me into the Hop Pole—I went in and took the prisoner in custody—another officer took the other man—they had been sitting, not together, but opposite each other, at the same table, which was not very wide—I took the prisoner away from the table—I did not see the last witness find anything, but he gave me this packet at the police-court—Mr. Lappidge gave me these two sixpences, one whole, and the other broken in two pieces—I found on the prisoner one halfpenny, that was all—I saw the other man searched, and 7 d. in copper was found on him—the other policeman is not here.
Prisoner. The other policeman looked under where I was sitting, and said he could see nothing.
Witness. The place was very dark, and the coins were in a dark paper—this is it—it was about half-past 6 o'clock.
WILLIAM WEBSTER . These sixpences are all counterfeit—the two uttered are of the dates of 1838 and 1844—of the five sixpences in the packet, here are three of the date of 1838, and they are from the same mould as one of those uttered, and two are of 1844, from the same mould as the other that was uttered.
Prisoner's Defence. If I had had them in my possession, do you think I should have gone in the other house with them? I went in the house with another man. I was looking to see if I could get any employ. I know nothing at all about the other man.
GUILTY .— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18600507-490]
490. JOHN DUDLEY (24), was indicted for a like offence.
MESSRS. POLAND and ORRIDGE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES WILLIAM GIBBS . I keep the Admiral Vernon in Upper Ground-street—On 28th April the prisoner came to the bar and asked for 1 1/2 d. worth of gin—he gave me a counterfeit shilling; I examined it and found it was bad; I told him so, and asked him if he had got any more—he said he had not, and he pulled out some good silver—he paid me for the gin with a good shilling—I gave him 10 1/2 d. change and returned him the bad shilling—he left, and shortly afterwards a constable brought him back, and he was searched in my bar—I saw the constable take one shilling from him—he showed it to me and I broke it in two pieces—I laid the pieces on the bar, and I don't know what became of them—the constable found no more bad money on him, and I allowed him to go.
Prisoner. Q. Was I not in liquor? A. You had been drinking a little, but was not tipsy.
EDWIN WARD ( Policeman, L 67). I took the prisoner on Saturday, 28th April—he was about thirty or forty yards from the last witness's house—there was no one in his company—I took him back and searched him—I found on him a bad shilling—I gave it to Mr. Gibbs—he broke it and put it down, and by some means the pieces got removed from the counter—I could not find them—I found some good silver on the prisoner, but no other bad money—he was allowed to go—he had been drinking, but was not drunk.
WILLIAM NICHOLSON . I keep the Anchor and Hope in Great Charlotte-street—On 28th April, between 6 and 7 in the evening, I saw the prisoner in my bar—my barmaid called my attention to him, and she gave me a bad shilling—I broke it in two—she said this man had just given it to her—I collared the prisoner, and before I took him out of the shop I saw Joseph Levy pick up a bad shilling which was lying on the floor between the prisoner's feet—he gave it to me, and he said, "I picked this up from between his feet"—the prisoner said nothing—I broke that shilling also, and took the prisoner to the station, and gave the pieces of the shillings to the constable there.
MARY ANN INGRAM . I am barmaid to the laat witness—I remember the prisoner coming on 28th April—he asked for 1 1/2 d. worth of gin, and gave me in payment a counterfeit shilling—it was between 6 and 7 o'clock—I called my master and gave him the shilling.
JOSEPH LEVY . I am a sawyer, and live in Eden-street, Broadwall, Lambeth—On Saturday evening, 28th April, I was at the Anchor and Hope—I saw the prisoner taken in custody by Mr. Nicholson—I picked up a counterfeit shilling from between the prisoner's feet—I gave it to Mr. Nicholson.
JOHN COOK ( Policeman, L 112). I searched the prisoner at the station—I found on him three half-crowns in good money, and one halfpenny—I took his right boot off, and found in it this counterfeit shilling—he was not drunk; he might have had a pint or two.
WILLIAM WEBSTER . These are all bad, and the shilling found in the boot is from the same mould as one of the others.
Prisoners Defence. At the time I was taken I was the worse for liquor, and was not at all in a fit state to be answerable for my actions. The publican states that he gave me the shilling back, and I went away with it, and the shilling found upon me. If I had intended to defraud I should have got rid of it. The other publican states I gave his barmaid a shilling, and he broke it; and his witness states that he picked up a shilling from the floor, and he broke that, and you will find there is only one shilling broken. One of the constables stated that I was rolling about the street, and if that is not to be drunk, I don't know what is. As to the piece found in my boot, I don't know how it came there. I am not guilty, as I knew not what I was doing.
GUILTY .— Confined Twelve Months.

WHITING — PLEADED GUILTY .

492. GEORGE BRIGGS (41) , Feloniously cutting Robert Styles on his head, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
MR. PLATT conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT STYLES . I live in Shaftsbury-street, Walworth, and am a blacksmith—On Saturday, 7th April, I was at my own house about 8 o'clock in the evening—I had been at home some time, and had had my tea—the prisoner came; my wife was out—I don't know whether she was gone to the prisoner's mother's—his mother's house is less than a quarter of a mile from mine—I was at home when a knock came to the door, and I was in the act of stirring the fire with this poker ( produced)—the prisoner's wife has worked at my house for my wife, who is a laundress, about twelve months—she was in the habit of working there about two or three times a week—she came to iron—on hearing this knock I got up to go to the door; when I got to the parlour-door I heard a noise in the passage—I did not open the street-door; the prisoner's wife opened that—I heard a noise as if a person fell down, or was knocked down—the poker was hanging down—it was not lifted up at all—the prisoner was there, and he said to me directly, "It is you, you b—r"—he struck me a blow with his fist, and said, "I mean to murder you to-night,"and snatched the poker out of my hand, and struck me with it five or six times—the blows were given me over the table—he seized me by the stock with one hand and struck me five or six times with the poker, and said, "I intend to murder you"—I lost a wonderful deal of blood—his wife came to my assistance, and said, "Don't murder the man"—between his wife and I we managed to put him out of the house, and he broke the panel of the door after the door was closed, and said, "I will go in and finish him"—I at last escaped by the back way into the next house—I afterwards went to a chemist and then to Dr. Howitt—I was under Dr. Howitt's care about nine days; I was not able to get out at all during that time—about two minutes after this happened I put a shawl over my head and went to the prisoner's mother's—I expected to find my wife there; I found the prisoner there; he knocked me down, and my arm was black and blue—he said he meant to finish me—his mother called out "Murder," and the prisoner was dragged away from me, and I was hurt a good deal.
Cross-examined by MR. SLEIGH Q. How long have you known the
prisoner's wife? A. It may be ten or twelve years—he did not complain five or six months ago, and beg and pray of me not to be continually having his wife at my house; he never did to me, nor to my knowledge—he came to my house, and every time almost he found her there—it has not been continual source of difference between me and this man—I never heard of it but once; that might be about three months back—I heard a little talk about it—he has not as far as three months back begged and prayed of me as a married man not to make his life miserable and wretched in my own wife's absence—my wife heard something about it at his mother's—my wife was the person to tell me, and I told my wife to get rid of her—it is the fact that this man's wife has been week after week in my house; she has been employed there—on this very occasion my wife was out, and this man's wife was in the house with me—she was at work—there was no other human being in the house at the time.
Q. At the time that she went to the door and you heard some noise, did she not cry out to you, "Bob! here is George?" A. No, I deny it; it is most false and gross—I did not hear her voice at all—I heard a slight scream in the passage—she did not call me Bob: she used to call me Mr. Styles—I went to the door and was struck—the prisoner and I did not have an altercation; we did not go into the back parlour before I was struck—it is not the fact that there was an altercation between him and me, and that I first struck him; that we went to the back parlour, and there was the place where the poker was wrenched from me—the prisoner's wife did not seize him by the collar and throw him down that I saw—he was never thrown down—she assisted me in wrenching the poker from his hand—she may have been in my house since, in my absence—she has never been in my house in my presence except the following morning—once and only once before this matter happened, she and I had been in a public-house—she came in while I was there, and I drank with her.
MR. PLATT. Q. Did you hear a scream at the door, and as if somebody had fallen? A. Yes; it was the scream of a woman—it was the prisoner's wife—I did not use any violence to him; not the slightest—I go out to work—I am out from a quarter before 6 o'clock in the morning till 7 at night—when this woman worked, she worked for my wife, and I saw nothing of her—there is no foundation for the rumour of my having been too intimate with her—I never in my life had any connexion with her—I have lived with Mrs. Styles as my wife for thirty years.
ANN STYLES . I am the prosecutor's wife—I am a laundress—I have been in the habit of employing the prisoner's wife to work for me for about twelve months—she was in the habit of coming sometimes two or three times a week—my husband works out and comes home in the evening—on 7th April, the prisoner came to me at my house about half-past 5 o'clock—he said to me, "Mrs. Styles, what business had you to tell my mother where lodged"—he was very violent, and I said, "Don't make a disturbance; I will go to your mother"—he said, "What time will you be there?" I said, "At 7 o'clock"—I went to his mother's house at 7 o'clock—he met me coming down, and went along with me—he said to me, "Did you leave my wife at your house?" and I said, "No"—I knew that she was not in my house when I left home—when I said No, he took hold of me by the shoulders and knocked me against the wall—I afterwards went home, and I found the door broken open and the place in Confusion and covered with blood—my husband was at the druggist's—while I and the prisoner were going to his mother's, he said he meant to beat my b—y husband into a
mummy if he found him with his wife—he said he was bent on murder that night.
Cross-examined. Q. Was he not in a very excited state? A. No, not at all; there was not the slightest excitement about him—he said all this in a quiet manner—when he said all these things, and took me by the shoulders and knocked me against the wall, he was not excited—I had known the prisoner's wife several years ago and had lost sight of her—it was not my husband who sought her and found where she was, and brought her to my house; but he tried to find her as well as I—my husband has not been in public-houses with this woman drinking, and the prisoner complaining of it—I did not tell my husband three months ago what I had heard—it was not talked of all about the neighbourhood how my husband was conducting himself with this man's wife—I heard it from the prisoner's mother—this woman has not been to my house since this matter occurred, never at all—she came to the door once; that is all—I can't tell how long previous to my meeting the prisoner I had left my house—the prisoner's wife had been there all that day—I did not leave her there—she had left in the evening to put her children to bed—she had not done her day's work; she was to come back as soon as she could—the prisoner never asked me if she had been there all day—I have seen her this morning—I and my husband and the prisoner's wife have not been at the public-house over the way during the last week—we have been there—I saw her there.
MR. PLATT. Q. In the whole of your life, did you see anything take place between your husband and that woman? A. Never.
HENRY WEAKFORD ( Policeman, M 133). On Saturday, 7th April, I received information, and took the prisoner in custody about half-past 8 o'clock in the evening at the Royal Oak public-house—his wife gave him in charge—on the way to the station, he said he wished he had a knife, he would run them both through—she was living with him—I afterwards found the prosecutor at the doctor's shop, wounded on the head—the prisoner appeared excited, but he was not drunk—this is the poker I found in the house—there were marks of blood on the knob.
Cross-examined. Q. What did you observe about him? A. He was excited certainly—he said if he had a knife he would have run them through—I searched him and he had a knife on him.
ENOCH DAWSON HOWITT . I live in Apollo-buildings, and am a surgeon—on 7th April, I was called to the police-station, I found the prosecutor there—he had five contused wounds on his scalp, three of them were from two to three inches long—the scalp was divided in five places—the wounds had gone down to the bone—he had bruises on his arms as if some person had stamped on him—the wounds turned out not to be dangerous—sometimes such wounds are of a serious character, and sometimes they are not—I have seen a man who fell on a fender and the scalp was divided from the eye to the head, but he has recovered and is living—these wounds might have been inflicted with this poker, decidedly—he was very faint and very low, but that I considered favourable—I considered that danger might ensue—the quantity of blood that he lost might be something of a benefit to him—the next day he was extremely exhausted and very faint.
Cross-examined. Q. Does it all depend on the habit of body? A. Yes, decidedly—he has not in the abstract suffered any very serious injury.
MR. SLEIGHT to HENRY WEAKFORD. Q. Have you seen the prosecutor and his wife, and the other woman, in company in public-houses? A. Yes; they were there, whether they were in company or not.
The prisoner's statement before the Magistrate was here read as follows:—"I was pretty well out of my mind when I did it—I can prove what I said about my wife—I caught another man in the room with her with his breeches down—I begged her to get away from the house, and while I was away she went, and went to live in the same place with that man—I met her afterwards and asked her where she lived, that I might see my children, and she would not tell me—Mrs. Styles came to my mother's and tried to get me out of my lodging—I went to her on the Saturday evening and asked her what she meant, she said she would meet me at my mother's—I went there and she said, 'You accused my husband of being along with your wife'—and I said, 'I am sorry, I can almost prove it'—she then told me her husband was no use to a woman; that when he came home from the Crimea he had the bad disorder, and that accounted for how mysteriously I had received the bad disorder and my wife too, about four years and a half ago—we then went to my mother's, and Mrs. Styles began to bluster—I said I had trouble enough—she said I accused my wife wrongfully, and if it was done it was not done in her house—I said she did not know that—I that my wife was at home when Mrs. Styles was out, and I said she was there now—she said she was not—I said I would go and see, I went and knocked at the door; my wife opened the door—I shoved her aside and said I would see who was there—as I shoved her side she called out, 'Styles, look out, it is George'—as I entered the back room he turned to the fire-place and took up the poker, and my wife seised me by the throat, and he struck me with the poker on the back of my hand—I took the poker from him and hit him, I don't know whether once, or twice, or a dozen times—I lost all control of myself at that time."
COURT to ANN STYLES. Q. Was there a fire in your back parlour? A. Yes; the ironing was going on there.
The prisoner received a good character.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Robert Malcolm Kerr, Esq.
[trialAccount t18600507-493]
493. JOHN GAY (34) , Feloniously cutting and wounding Mary Ann Gay upon her neck and throat, with intent to murder her. Second Count. To do her some grievous bodily harm.
MR. METCALFE conducted the Prosecution.
MARY ANN GAY . I am the prisoner's wife—on 5th April, I was living apart from him at Mrs. Clement's, 5, Park-lane, Clapham—I did live at 4, Mill-row, Clapham, and I went from there to Mrs. Clements—I went to try to get a lodging—no one would take me in on account of my husband—on 5th April, I saw my husband—I had been washing and I saw him against the door of the lodging—he asked me to give him a little hat brush, and I said yes, and he followed me into the passage and asked if I would have a little beer—I said, "No"—I went up stairs and got the hat brush and gave it him—I went out and left him there—I met Mrs. Clements, and I told her he was in the house—I returned with Mrs. Clements—I went in doors and sat down, and he sat down by the side of me—he went and got some beer and asked me to drink with him, and I did so—Mrs. Clements asked him to go, and I asked him myself to go—he said he had very comfortable lodgings—I said I was very glad of it, and I told him not to trouble me and I would not trouble him—he was going, and he came to the foot of the stairs and asked me to shake hands with him, and I did so—he then asked me to stop the night with him—I said,"No, you said you had a comfortable lodging, go to your lodging"—he then said he would stop—Mrs. Clements
said she hoped he would not come there, it was a quiet place and we did not want to have a noise—he again asked me to stop—I said, "No," and he left—on the following morning about 6 o'clock, I was getting my breakfast, Mrs. Clements was gone to work, and Mr. Clements was out; there were two children in the house—while getting my breakfast a knock came at the door; I went to the door and it was my husband—he asked me for a bag which he had left the night before—I said he had not left a bag there, I supposed he had left it in his room at night and had forgotten it—I looked round for the bag and I said there was no one there—he then asked me for some water—I said, "Why do you not go to your lodging"—he then said with an oath that he would cut my throat, and before the words were out of his mouth I felt the cut at my throat—I struggled with him very much and defended the cut off with my hand, and he cut the leaders of my left hand—I fell and he fell on me—he rose, and he was endeavouring to cut my throat—I fell down and was on my knees—I struggled and got the razor from him, and he put his fingers in my throat to tear the wound open—the wound bled very much indeed; it bled much more after that—my husband got up and ran away—I was very much exhausted—I hallooed murder as long as I could, and some gentleman came and said I had cut my throat as I had the razor in my hand—I said no, my husband had done it—I saw Mrs. Clements and asked her to run for a doctor, and I was taken to the hospital—I don't know of any cause for this—my husband has not been happy with me for the last three years—I believe it was jealousy, but I believe the jealousy has been on the wrong side—I have had two warrants and a summons for him for his ill usage, and the last time he said if I had him brought up again, he would give me something for it—that is about a month ago.
Cross-examined by MR. TINDAL ATKINSON Q. How long have you been married to him? A. Eleven years on the 27th of last December—I did not leave him by agreement; he left me—he left me with the furniture in the house—he left me in debt—he went to lodgings—I removed the articles away from my own house—he asked me whether I had sold the things, and I did not tell him—I said, I had the things there, but I had to pay for them—we have not been happy together for the last three years—it was at my suggestion that he left me—when he came to me this time he did not appear to be in liquor—I observed something about him that I did not like—there was a wildness about him—it was quite unusual for me to see him look so—I did not go and leave him three years ago; he left me—there was a lodger staying with me when my husband was with me; that was Mary Ann Donald—I never had any male person lodge there—I never left the prisoner with any male person; I don't recollect that I did—I did not leave him three years ago for a little while; he left me—I did not live with any man that time—I never lived with a man since I have known my husband—I know a man of the name of Metcalfe, he never stayed with me any time—I never had any improper connexion with any man since I was married.
SARAH CLEMENTS . I live in Park-lane, Clapham—my husband is a carrier, and I go out to work as a laundress—Mrs. Gay was living with me—on 5th April I saw the prisoner in the street, he Baid, "Is that Mrs. Clements?"—I said, "Yes"—he said he wanted to see Polly, meaning his wife—I went back and there was the prisoner; he sat down by the side of his wife and pressed her very much to have some beer, and I wished her to take some because I was afraid he would make a noise—he fetched a pot of porter himself and she and I partook of it—my husband did not drink any—the
I prisoner then wanted to fetch more beer, but I wished him to go—I said to I him, "Go"—he rose up to go, and then he asked his wife if she would let I him stop there that night—she said, "No" and I said, "No"—he went up the stairs and said he would stop with her—I told him she was only a I lodger and he could not—he came down again and went out at the door—I the next morning I saw Mrs. Gay bleeding from a wound in her throat—there was a great quantity of blood came from her, not so much in the kitchen, but in the passage there was a quantity of blood—I ran to the I doctor—the doctor came and dressed her throat, and she was taken to the I hospital—when I came back from the doctor the prisoner saw me in Acre-lane, and said I kept a regular b—y house and encouraged his w—e to I come there; there is not the least ground for saying I keep a house of that description—I never knew of any man living with the prisoner's wife; she I is a hard-working woman—I had seen the prisoner before—I have not heard him before make complaints of his wife.
Cross-examined. Q. Is it a delusion to say that you keep an improper house? A. Yes, it is—I don't know whether the prisoner was very wild when he was talking in that way, I did not look at him much.
WILLIAM YOUNG . I live in Park-place, No. 5 is at the back of me—about three minutes before 6 o'clock that morning, I was in my garden and heard screams—I went to No. 5 and saw Mrs. Gay, she was at the end of the passage on the edge of the kerb throwing her hands about and seemingly in great pain—she had at that time a razor in her hand—I took the razor from her and gave it to the policeman—the razor was bloody—I saw the prisoner about five minutes afterwards, I saw him wash his hands in a pond—I walked by his side till I met a policeman, and he gave himself up—after I gave up the razor at the station he gave up the sheath and said it belonged to it.
THOMAS NEWMAN ( Police-sergeant, V 25). About ten minutes after 6 o'clock that morning, I was on duty at Clapham—I saw the prisoner followed by the last witness and several others; they said, "Stop that man, he has cut his wife's throat"—the prisoner said, "I don't want to be stopped, I have done it, I was coming to the station to give myself up"—after I took him to the station, I went to the house and saw Mrs. Gay on the floor, the doctor was attending her—the place was bloody all over; the passage, the floor, and the walls—this is the razor I received from the last witness—the prisoner took this sheath of it out of his own pocket at the station—I think he said, "This is the sheath."
SARAH WILLIAMS . I am bar-maid at the Oxford-arms, Clapham—about 5 o'clock on the morning of the 6th April the prisoner came there—he had some brandy and ale—he remained till about a quarter before 6 o'clock; he then left and was away about ten minutes, he came back and asked for more brandy—he said, "I am going to pay you for it, this is the last I shall ever drink; I have out my wife's throat, I have come here to wait for the policeman to take me"—I begged him not to stop—his hands were covered with blood—he said, "Look here"—he just went in the parlour and then went away.
ELIZA DRUMMOND . I live at 39, Park-place, opposite to where Mrs. Gay lived—on 6th April, about 6 o'clock, I heard several screams—I looked out of window and saw the prisoner and his wife struggling in the passage—his wife was upon her knees, and the prisoner seemed as if he was getting up from his wife—he was at her back—he rushed into the road and then ran up the street.
JAMES BUNKART . I am house surgeon at Guy's Hospital—on the morning
of 6th April, Mary Ann Gay was brought in—I did not see her till about 11 o'clock—she had two incised wounds on her throat, one about four inches long, extending from the left ear to the middle of the throat—the other was about two inches long, about an inch and a half below it, towards the centre of the throat; I should think about half an inch deep—they were cut from left to right—the principal depth was on the left side of the throat—she was not in danger—I found some small wounds on her hands.
Crost-examined. Q. They were not of depth enough to injure life? A. No, they were not—the principal wound was on the side of the neck—the cuts on the hands were such as would be made by attempting to defend the throat.
JURY. Q. Did either of the wounds present the appearance of having been torn? A. I could not judge that.
COURT. Q. Were the ends of the wounds torn? A. No.
GUILTY .— DEATH recorded.
[trialAccount t18600507-494]
494. WILLIAM DUNN (20), and SARAH DUNN (40) , Robbery on Jane Bellamy, and stealing from her person a shawl, and 15 s. in money, the property of John Bellamy.
MR. CAARTEN conducted the Prosecution.
JANE BELLAMY . I am the wife of John Bellamy, of 115, Union-street, Borough—about a quarter to 12 o'clock on the evening of 21st April I was with a Mrs. Mauler at the Red Lion public-house, very nearly opposite to where I live—I had not been in there more than while I got served with my supper beer, which I had gone for—I gave the barman a sovereign—the prisoners were there when I went in—there were a great many people there—I had known the prisoners before, by living in the neighbourhood—I received 19 s. 6 d. change from the barman—the prisoners saw me receive it—having received it I left the Red Lion with Mrs. Mauler—the prisoners followed us out instantly—I was crossing the road to go to my house, with my beer in my hand—the male prisoner came up to me, and asked me what I meant by calling his father moucher —I had not called his father moucher —he said if I had made use of such an expression my husband was to come out and show what he could do—my husband came up out of his shop—he is a boot and shoe maker—we have been residing in that house three years—when he came up the male prisoner instantly attacked him, struck him, and they both fell—the prisoner got up off the ground, turned round, and struck me—I was about two yards from where the scuffle took place between him and my husband—I tried to help my husband off the ground—I did not strike the prisoner in doing that—I had not struck him at all before he came and struck me in the face—I had a black eye, and my forehead was all scratched—I fell on the ground—at this time I had the change of the money in my right hand—having struck me, he made a grasp at my hand, and all the money was taken from me but 4 s. 6 d.—he held my hand, wrenched it open, and took the money out, but some of the silver fell on the ground—he picked some of it up, ran away, and I pursued him—the female prisoner did not do anything to me, before she stole my shawl—nobody else did anything to me whilst I was on the ground—I ran after the male prisoner, and came up to him at Princes-street, which is in Union-street—he then struck me again a violent blow in the face—I fell, and he ran away—I got up and pursued him—I called out "Thief," and "Police"—when I was down the second time the female prisoner came up, and assisted
in pulling me about till she pulled the shawl off my back—she is his mother—she then ran away to the end of the buildings where they live—I could see her—she threw the shawl to another son, who is not in custody—he was standing at the end of the buildings where they live—I cannot say whether that son is older or younger than the one at the bar—the son went with the shawl up the buildings, where they live—I had got up from the ground at this time—some man, I don't know who, was holding the male prisoner during this time, about thirty or forty yards, I should think, from where I had been knocked down the second time—he had run away after I was knocked down the second time—I saw him given into the custody of Ireland, the policeman—I never got back any of the money that was taken out of my hand.
Cross-examined by MR. LILLEY. Q. Was this a quarter to 12 on Saturday night? A. Yes—I am sure I had only gone into the Red Lion a few minutes—I had been in before in the evening for a pint and a half of porter, as I always do about 9 o'clock—I had not been in more than once before—I had known the prisoners by sight about four years—I never drank with them—I was never in their company, except going in and out there for my beer—my husband did not go with me to the Red Lion—I do not recollect taking off my bonnet and shawl, and offering a little fight, after the young man came up and asked me about mouchur —a person took my shawl off, and the remains of my bonnet, which was torn—that was close to my own door—they were off—as soon as this altercation took plape a large number of people collected round there; there always is—I saw the male prisoner, but none of the others, picking up some of the money that went on the ground—I did not try to help the male prisoner on to the ground—I am quite sure I did not touch him—I am positive I did not raise my hand at all—the prisoner and my husband fell on the ground together—I did not then raise my foot, or anything of the sort—I swear that I took no part in the struggle at all—I was exactly opposite the Red Lion when I lost my shawl.
COURT. Q. When the prisoner was taking the money out of your hand, where was your other hand? A. It might have been as it is now—I was down on the ground when he took it out of my hand—he wrenched my hand open—I did not use my other hand at all—I was holding his brother, who is not in custody—I did not grasp at any person—my husband was persuaded to go back and take care of the shop, for fear we should have been robbed—our shop is all exposed—there is no window—he went and left me in the prisoner's hands—I resisted when the female prisoner came up to take my shawl—she was using most abusive language—there was a great crowd—nobody helped to stop this—I am quite sure that I had been into no other public-house that evening but the Red Lion—I took a part, and my husband took a part of the pint and a half of porter that I got in the early part of the evening—neither I nor my husband have ever had any words with the female prisoner during the four yeare we have known them—I had two shawls on—a person living opposite took the large shawl and my bonnet, and minded them till I sent for them on Sunday morning—I don't know whether that woman is here—I was perfectly sober.
ANN MAULER . I am the wife of Joseph Mauler, of 20, Pepper-street, Union-street, Borough, a bellhanger and gasfitter—he is employed by his father—on Saturday night, 21st April, I was with the last witness in the Red Lion; only while she was being served with a pot of half-and-half—I saw the sovereign changed, and the change handed to her—the prisoners were both in the public-house at the time—I knew them by sight—they
were never acquaintances of mine—Mrs. Bellamy and I left the public-house—when we got into the road the male prisoner came up and said to Mrs. Bellamy, "Why did you call my father moucher?" she said, "No; I was not talking about you at all; I was talking to my friend"—I don't know the meaning of moucher —Mrs. Bellamy had not used the word in my presence—the prisoner said, "Because, if you were, I would take it out of your b—y husband"—with that he struck Mrs. Bellamy—the prisoner's mother was with him then—Mr. Bellamy came across, and asked what wag the matter—I did not see him before the male prisoner had struck Mrs. Bellamy—it was a violent blow in the face, with his fist—it stunned her, and knocked her down—she fell on the ground, and then the male prisoner wrenched hold of her hand, and took some of the silver out, and part of it dropped on the ground—he stooped to piok it up—I laid hold of his hand, and said, "Don't rob the woman of her money, give it to her"—he said, "What money? I have got none"—I am positive I saw him take the money, and pick it up from the ground—no one interfered with Mrs. Bellamy while she was on the ground—she got up and ran after the male prisoner, whose brothers I know by sight—two of them were there while Mm Bellamy was on the ground—I think they are older than he—they interfered, and tried to get him away from Mrs. Bellamy while she was on the ground—she had not got hold of him—they only said, "Come away," they did not do anything—while Mrs. Bellamy was on the ground the first time the female prisoner struck her on the face—I can't say whether Mrs. Bellamy saw her do so—there was a crowd of people—it is a very bad neighbourhoodz—the male prisoner ran on, and then turned round and struck Mrs. Bellamy, who was pursuing him—she got up to him near Princes-street, which is five or six yards from the place where she was first knocked down—he struck her and knocked her down a second time—while she was on the ground, I saw the female prisoner tear her shawl off and give it to one of her sons who had been present at the first transaction—it was a silk shawl, with large flowers on—the female prisoner then ran up the court—the prisoners do not live very far from where Mrs. Bellamy was knocked down the second time—I saw the male prisoner stopped by a gentleman—after he knocked Mrs. Bellamy down the second time, he continued his flight till the gentleman stopped him—he had got about four or five yards from where he knocked her down the second time—Mrs. Bellamy cried out, "Stop thief!" and I saw him stopped and given in charge to a policeman.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you lived in the neighbourhood? A. I was brought up in it—I have known the prisoners and their family, by sight, eleven years—I can't say whether, during that time, they have been living in the same neighbourhood—I have seen them about—I did not know where they lived—I did not, nor do I now know the buildings down where they lived—Mrs. Bellamy asked me to go across with her to get her supper beer—I live about two or three minutes' walk from the Red Lion—Mrs. Bellamy asked me to go, because she and I were next door neighbours—I had come direct from ray home, and was going out to market to buy what I wanted—I had not been to the Red Lion or to any other public-house before, that evening—I am positive that I took nothing at all when I was in the Red Lion—I only know the prisoners by sight—I have never been in their company or drank with them.
COURT. Q. You say Mrs. Bellamy asked you to go across with her to get her supper beer? A. Yes—she had not been drinking—she was perfectly sober—there were not the least symptoms of disagreement in the
public-house—the Bellamy's keep a shop, and I suppose Mr. Bellamy went back to his shop because he could not leave it—I do not know whether he left his wife to fight it out—I did not see what he did—I saw some scuffling between him and the prisoner—he was knocked down two or three times, and after that, I think, he went back to his shop; oh, I forgot, a boy came up and said to him that there was a customer in the shop—this shindy might have taken twenty-five minutes.
JAMES VERNON . I am a traveller, and live at 84, Westminster-road—I was in Union-street, Borough, on Saturday evening, 21st April, about five minutes to 12—I did not know any of the parties in this case—I saw the prosecutrix and Mrs. Mauler—they stepped on to the foot path and were passing immediately before me—I did not see the house they came from—they stepped on to the foot path as if they came across the road—I saw the male prisoner come up immediately—he assaulted the prosecutrix, and pulled off his coat, and said he would fight her husband or any other person she liked to bring, and immediately upon that he made a rush at her, struck her, and knocked her down, and I saw him make a grasp at one of her hands—it was in the face, I think, that he struck her—he laid hold of her hand—they both fell down, and money fell from the proseoutrix's hand—I did not see what became of it; it scattered on the ground—there were two others, I believe, with the prisoner; they were all together, and there was a scramble for the money—both the prisoners, and two or three others took part in the scramble—I cannot say whether the male prisoner succeeded in getting any of the money—I saw Mrs. Bellamy on the ground—I did not notice anything done to her by anybody while on the ground—she and the male prisoner got up almost together—the prisoner attempted to run away—he ran for perhaps four or five yards—Mrs. Bellamy pursued him and he immediately turned round, struck her in the face, and knocked her down—he then ran away—a crowd immediately collected; I did not go into the crowd, but I saw a policeman coming up, who went to them—when I first saw Mrs. Bellamy she had on a dark shawl which she took off, but under that she had a light shawl, apparently a silk one—I saw her after she had been knocked down the second time—she had no shawl at all on then—I should say three or four minutes elapsed between my losing sight of her, and seeing her the second time.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see her also take her bonnet off? A. Yes—I did not see her husband at all, till after the male prisoner was given in custody and it was all over—there was not a considerable crowd immediately—I do not know the Bed Lion.
COURT. Q. You stood on the pavement and looked on? A. Yes—I should not think it lasted above five minutes—I do not know whether Mrs. Bellamy's bonnet and shawl were loose, and she gave them to some one for safety; but she pursued the prisoner—she was knocked down the first time, and she immediately threw them off—I could form no opinion as to whether there had been any drinking—Mrs. Bellamy appeared sober—I did not see her husband knocked down at all—the male prisoner made a rush at Mrs. Bellamy's hand before she fell down.
JOHN CLEMENTS IRELAND ( Policeman, M 217). I was on duty on this Saturday night in the neighbourhood of Union-street, Borough—I saw a row in the street, and a crowd of people—Mr. Vernon said something to me—I went up to the prisoner and he was given in my custody by two men.
DENIS SCANNELL ( Policeman, M 177). On Sunday morning, about half-past 12, at her own house, which is in a court leading out of Union-street,
the female prisoner was given in my custody for stealing a shawl, and being concerned with her son in robbing Mrs. Bellamy of her shawl—when I told her the charge she said she was an honest woman and knew nothing about the shawl; that if I went up stairs I should find the shawl there—I went up stairs with Mr. Bellamy—while going up stairs I heard a rush at the street door—I found no shawl up there—there was another constable in the passage—the place was very dark.
GUILTY — Confined Eight Days each.
Before Mr. Baron Channell.
[trialAccount t18600507-495]
495. WILLIAM HARPER (26) , Feloniously killing and slaying Thomas Faye.
MR. SHARPE conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE STOWER . I am a cab-driver of 4, Parliament-street, St. George's Market, Southwark—I knew the deceased Thomas Faye, a coal-porter, he was about forty years of age—on Friday, 16th March, I saw him at the Elephant and Castle public-house, near Vauxhall Railway-station, about half-past 9 or twenty minutes to 10 o'clock—the prisoner was also there—they both appeared as if they had been drinking, they were rather the worse for liquor—I heard them quarrelling, but did not know what it was about—one of them proposed to go out and fight; I cannot say which, but they were both willing, and I tried to prevent them—I held the deceased and prevented him from going out: after a little struggle I said, "Well; if you will go, you must go and take your chance"—they both left and went under the arches, somewhere: into the grounds of the station. I remained some few minutes, and then went towards the arches, where my cab was standing for hire when the trains came in—I heard a noise like a fall, and went and saw Thomas Faye fall, just as I got to the spot—he laid on his back very quiet—I did not lift him up—he did not speak or make any movement—the prisoner was about three yards in front of his feet—he was lifted up—I did not see him taken away—I went to the Elephant and Castle, remained there perhaps two minutes, and then went away to my cab.
Cross-examined by MR. DICKIE. Q. Had both the men had a good deal of drink? A. Yes; but they could walk—Faye was a very stout healthy man—I have heard that he was in the habit of sleeping in the gas factory—he was very dirty in his habits—I have never seen the waiters at the tap-room carry water for him to wash himself—he was often at the public-house.
DENNIS HARRINGTON . I am a labourer of James'-place, Fore-street, Lambeth—I was at the Elephant and Castle on Friday, 16th March, and heard the prisoner and deceased quarrelling about work—I could not understand exactly what was said, but after some time they agreed to go out and fight—I do not know which proposed it, one was as willing as the other—they were both under the influence of liquor—they left the Elephant and Castle together to fight—I remained behind some little time—I then went to see what was taking place—( The witness was cautioned that he need not say anything tending to criminate himself,)—I do not want to say anything—I will speak the truth—I took no part whatever in encouraging them to fight—I saw them sparring together, and after that I saw the prisoner lay hold of Faye by the legs and chuck him on his head—he fell on the back of his head, as far as I could see—I said to the prisoner that it was a shame, and he struck me—I struck him back again—I did not see Faye afterwards till I saw him on Monday night—he was helped up and led away—I cannot say whether he walked—he seemed sensible—he was not bleeding that
I saw—I did not see the back of his head, or whether he had any wound—I saw the prisoner on the Tuesday following, and he told me he was very sorry for what had happened.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know the deceased? A. Yes: I do not believe he had any place of abode—he lived in a gas factory, and I believe he used to sleep there—I do not believe he has slept in a bed for a couple of years—I do not know that he slept among the ashes—he was a very dirty man, and was constantly drinking. I have seen him wash himself in the public room.
JOHN JAMES PARSONS . I am a labourer of Kingswood-place, South Lambeth—on Friday, 16th March, about half-past 9 o'clock, I was returning from work, and saw the prisoner and the deceased come out of the Elephant and Castle—they crossed over the road and went into one of the railway arches—there were one or two others with them—I stood at the gate of the Vauxhall station and looked in—it was within the enclosure—I stood there two or three minutes, and saw them pull off their smock frocks for the purpose of fighting—they stood sparring for a minute or two, and then the prisoner rushed at Faye with his head downwards, caught hold of his legs, and turned him over—he fell on the back of his head very heavily—he was the heavier man of the two, and about forty years of age—he laid there for a few seconds, groaning, and was picked up—he stood up as well as he could, and then Harper went at him a second time—there was more sparring, and he went in to him and turned him over in the same way, and the fall was much heavier than the first—he fell on the back of his head—they were both intoxicated—I remained two or three minutes, but he was not sensible—he never spoke; he groaned very much, and was dragged away to the Elephant and Castle—I then went for a policeman, but could not find one, and when I came back I saw the prisoner and Harrington fighting.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you ever seen either of them before? A. I had seen the deceased—I cannot say that I have seen the prisoner, but am quite sure he is the man—they passed me on the opposite side going to the arches, but I could not hear what they said to each other—the deceased was much heavier and taller, and apparently a much more powerful man than the prisoner—they were both intoxicated.
MR. SHARPE. Q. How long was all this taking place under the arches? A. I should think twenty minutes—I did not see the back of the deceased's head and cannot say whether there was any wound.
JOHN FORRESTER . I am a horse dealer, of 1, Freeport-place, Wandsworth-road; on Friday, 16th March, I was at the Elephant and Castle when the deceased was brought there; he was like half tipsy; stupid—I looked at his head and saw just a little cut at the back of it, about as large as my nail, and clotted with blood—I can't exactly say the size, on account of the hair—after I had washed it he came to a little bit, and I tried to persuade him to go to the hospital, but he would not—there was a small quantity of blood, enough for me to see—it was such a blow as might be occasioned by his falling with the back of his head on a stone or hard substance—I stopped with him till 11 or 12, I left him then; I saw him again on Saturday morning; he told me that he had been into the gas works again to sleep—he seemed very bad and complained of his head—I was with him about an hour, and tried to persuade him again to go to the hospital in a cab, but he would not, and I did not see him again till Monday, the 19th, at 10 or 11 o'clock—I then got a cab and took him to the workhouse; he appeared very bad—he seemed fixed and stupid—I kept going every day in and out
to see him, till within a quarter of an hour of his death—I saw his body at the Coroner's Inquest.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know that he was in the habit of sleeping at the gas house? A. Yes; I do not know that he slept among the cinders—he was three-parts of his time at the Elephant and Castle—he might have been employed one day out of two or three—he was a great drinker.
MICHAEL LEWIS . I am an inmate and wardsman of Lambeth Poorhouse—on Monday, the 19th, about 5 o'clock, the deceased was brought there—he appeared rather lightheaded, and complained of his head—he was left in my charge—he could walk and stand, but he went to bed half an hour after he came, and complained of a violent pain in his head—I looked at the back of his head; there was a cut or wound about an inch long, with a piece of sticking plaister on it—I took the old plaister off and put new on about an hour after he was brought in—the medical officer was sent for, who ordered him to go to bed and have all nourishment, wine, bread and butter and tea, and whatever he could take, which I gave him; but he could not take much—he was put into a bath and cleaned, he came to me—he complained next morning of a violent pain across his head, in front, and seemed much worse—he had taken the medicine that I gave him—he had constant fits till Saturday the 4th, when he died about a quarter past 4 in the afternoon—the first fit he had was on Wednesday night—he was a little sensible when he came out of the fits, but he had no time before the next fit took him.
HENRY ST. JOHN BUTLER . I am house surgeon and medical officer of Lambeth Workhouse—on Monday, 19th March, I was sent for to attend upon Thomas Faye, about 5 o'clock in the afternoon—I found him in the bath room washing himself—I accosted him—his replies were confused and incoherent—I asked him what was the matter with him; he said, "Nothing"—my attention was directed by the bath man to his head—I examined it, and found a slight wound about three-quarters of an inch long; it appeared to be incised, and such as might have been inflicted with a flint or a sharp stone, or any sharp instrument—I removed the plaister, and found it was in process of healing—in my opinion a violent fall on the back of the head on a stone would probably produce it.
COURT. Q. Could you tell whether the wound had bled much? A. I should presume not; there was a little clotted blood on the hair—it was a sort of wound more likely to be caused by a fell than a blow; the small quantity of blood would lead me to that conclusion.
MR. SHARPE. Q. Was he a heavy person? A. A remarkably heavy man, a very fine man—his being heavy would be more likely to cause a wound of this kind—after I had examined him in the bath room he was received into a ward by my orders—I saw him again in about an hour; he was then leaning his head in his hand—I spoke to him, and he complained of his head—I ordered his head to be shaved in order that I might make a more complete examination—that was done—I ordered him a mustard plaister—I did not at that time detect any injury to the brain, though I suspected it—the wound had only penetrated the scalp—the pupils of his eyes were somewhat dilated; that, and other symptoms, led me to the conclusion that he was suffering from concussion of the brain—he said he had lost his appetite, and complained of great oppression in breathing—I had him put to bed, and saw him twice next day—I had received no information as to the particulars of this fight, but from the information I received, and from the symptoms, I judged he was suffering from compression of the brain, and treated him for that.
COURT. Q. If he had been injured and tripped up twice bo that he fell on the back of his head, would that have accounted for what you saw? A. It might—I don't think the fact of his having been of intemperate habits would make any difference in this injury—by concussion I simply mean stunning, from which a person recovers shortly; compression is the result of the slow oozing of blood on the surface of the brain, which gradually causes that effect—a patient is less likely to recover from that than from concussion—it is a small oozing from the brain or one of its vessels.
MR. SHARPE. Q. I believe on the Tuesday evening he was half delirious? A. Yes; those symptoms kept on increasing—he had an epileptic fit on Wednesday evening; those continued to the time of his death—I was not with him at the exact moment of his death, but I was shortly before—I afterwards made a post-mortem examination—I first opened the head—I found a quantity of extravasated blood on the surface of the brain, and towards the base—I collected it; there was about three ounces of fluid blood—I cannot exactly define how much there was at the base of the brain; the blood there was lying on the right side, at the back of the head, on a level with the ear—it commenced exactly at the cut which was just at the apex, and went to the back part; it had gravitated down to the base of the brain—the blood had all proceeded from an artery which was wounded; that would be likely in time to cause death—I saw the extent to which it had gravitated, and it must have destroyed life—it would be likely to cause epileptic fits with other symptoms—the ruptured artery on the brain was the immediate cause of death—I examined the skull, it was unusually thin—if he had been tripped up by his legs and fallen, it might have caused both the extravasated blood and the wound; it is a common cause—I attach no importance to the scalp wound, it was purely external; both the external and the internal rupture of the artery were, at the same part—the probability of such a rupture would be increased by reason of the heavy weight of the man, and would be still further increased by the remarkable thinness of the skull; the thinner the skull is the more the external violence would be felt—I examined the brain, and found it in an unusually healthy condition; there was nothing in it to cause death, except the rupture of the vessel and the blood in the brain—I found a fracture of the skull extending from the apex, or about an inch below it, to about an inch from the cut, extending through the temporal bone right through the bones of the ear in a diagonal direction; it was a linear fracture without depression—I should attribute such a fracture more to a fall than a blow.
COURT. Q. You say there was a crack, but no depression of the bone on the brain? A. Yes—a sudden knock would cause it, which would be increased by the man being heavy and his skull not being thick—I attribute death to the effusion of blood—we have many cases where persons recover from simple fracture of the skull; if it had only been a linear fracture the patient might have recovered—seeing the blood that oozed from the rupture, I am of opinion that that was the cause of death.
Cross-examined. Q. When the case first came before you, did you think it was a serious case? A. Yes—I did not feel certain what had been the nature of the injury he had received, or whether he had received any—that there was some effusion of the brain I felt certain, but had no history before me—I did not consider the little cut at the back of the head of any consequence by itself—I felt certain that a fall had caused it—I was not aware, during life, that there had been any fracture of the skull—I think it more likely to have arisen from a blow or a series of blows—the body was generally
healthy—the cause of death was solely and entirely to be found in the brain—I should say that a man must fall at least four feet to cause that rupture of an artery of the brain—a man drunk and falling down might do it.
COURT. Q. Supposing a man were standing, and his legs were tripped up, would he fall four feet? A. Yes, this man would, for he was six feet high—his head would fall four or five feet, I should say.
MR. DUKE to FORRESTER. Q. Did the deceased at any time challenge you to fight? A. When he was tipsy he challenged any one—he was very quarrelsome when he was tipsy, but when he was sober he was a very quiet man.
GUILTY .— Confined One Month.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18600507-495a]
495. CHARLES VOWLES (25) , Feloniously cutting and wounding James Luscombe with intent to murder him. Second count. With intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
MESSRS. METCALFE and BEASELEY conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES LUSCOMEE ( Policeman, P.) On Wednesday night, 11th April, I was on duty in Pomeroy-street—the prisoner was given into my custody by a female who said in his presence that he had assaulted her—there were three marks of violence on her face—I told him he must go to the station with me—he said, "What! for two such b—y stinking wh—s as them; as for you, I will lay you dead in the road if you attempt to put a hand upon me"—I persuaded him to go to the station quietly, which he refused to do—I took hold of him by the arm—he was trying to get his arm away from me, and struck me a blow on the face—I took him by the cuff of his coat, and put my other hand inside his cravat and said, "Now then, for what you were going to do"—I took him to the station and he was discharged, the woman was obliged to take out a summons—I saw him again about five minutes after he left the station, near the Montpelier public-house, Queen's-road, Peckham—I was passing and he said, "Oh, you b— smot; you have not got your wish to-night"—I told him it was not my wish to lock him up, and I had not said anything about it at the station; that I did not tell them he had assaulted me—he said, "No, you did not; will you come and have some beer?"—I said, "No, thank you"—I walked away—I saw him again about 12 o'clock at night, outside the Victoria beer-shop, in Pomeroy-street—I was still on my beat—he was halloing and making a great noise—I went down—several of his mates were there, and when he saw me he said, "This is the b—that took me to the station to-night, I should like to give him a doing "—I persuaded him to go home, and think no more of it; two of his mates got hold of him—he said, "Let me go, I will give that b— a doing to-night"—that was all he said that night—I saw him again the following night about 12 o'clock, in front of the Victoria public-house—Cox, a constable, was with me—I heard several people talking—the prisoner came out, I did not know he was there—he began halloing and said "I wonder where this b— snot is who took me to the station last night, if I could see him I would soon do for him"—one of his mates pointed across the road and said, "There stand two"—the prisoner then ran across the road and said, "This is the b—that took me to the station last night," pointing to me—he came up quickly and said, "You are the one that took me to the station last night, are you not?"—I said, "Yes; have not you forgotten that yet?"—I persuaded him to think nothing more home—I said, "That is all settled as far as you and I are
concerned"—he said, "Yes, you b—, I will settle you yet"—I persuaded him to go home—he went a short distance up the street—I stopped there—he came back in about five minutes with one of his mates, and walked round me—his mate said, "Never mind, Charlie, we' are in our own street; we can do as we like here, and as for those b— snots, it would take forty of them to take us to the station"—I said nothing—the prisoner came up to me and kept threatening me—I persuaded him to go away home—he shoved up against me at one time, and then went away up the street towards his own home—I did not follow him for nearly five minutes, and then that was the direction I was going in—I saw him standing at his own door, and passed him—he followed me almost directly—I was one or two paces in front of him—he kept on threatening me—I persuaded him to go in—he said, "Certainly, will you allow me to smoke my cigar?" putting it in his mouth—I said, "Certainly"—he said, "How can I smoke it without a light?"—I said, "You can get a light inside"—he said, "I think I have got a match here," putting his hand in his right pocket; and instead of taking out a match, he took out a knife, looked over his right shoulder as if he was going to strike a match, opened the knife, and I heard the spring of it—he threw his arm out to it's full length and aimed at my cheek or throat—I turned my head round over my shoulder, put it down, and received the stab just behind the left shoulder—he then rushed at me, and struck me with the knife in his hand—I had got my staff out and I struck at him and guarded the knife off—he rushed at me a third time in the same way, and I struck his hand and guarded the knife off—the fourth time I struck his hand and knocked the knife out of it, upon that he turned away from me as if he was going to run down the street—I saw my mate come up the street, and I called out, "Mind the knife, he has stabbed me"—he was running as fast as he could—I saw him strike my mate and knock him down in the road—I followed—he ran down the street until he got to the passage from Pomeroy-street, and just as he was going through, I made a grasp at him, missed my aim, and fell over the posts—I got up, followed him, and caught him in Cameron-street—Cox was there—I sprang my rattle for assistance, and he was taken to the station—my shoulder had a very severe stab, and bled very much—I have not the use of it yet—I have been under medical care for some time—this knife ( produced) was picked up—it is the one the prisoner had in his hand—I told the constable Holdaway whereabouts it was that I had been struck.
Cross-examined by MR. LILLEY. Q. At the time you saw him on the night of the 11th was not he partially in liquor when the two women came up? A. Certainly he had been drinking—one of his mates was in his company—I do not know whether his name is Barraclough—I cannot say whether he followed to the station—I saw him outside the door when we came out—the prisoner was discharged at the station at a quarter to eleven—it was about twelve when I saw him in Pomeroy-street—I did not see him come out of the Victoria—I saw him outside, and I had not seen him go in—this was Tuesday in Easter week—I know England's manufactory—I have heard since that the prisoner worked there—I do not know that the men were off work at that time on account of the holidays—there were several other men, who I call his mates, outside the Victoria—he crossed the road to me—I am quite sure a woman spoke to me while I was there, I did not know her, I have not seen her since—she did not advise me not to interfere with the man, and not to take any notice of him, for he was very drunk—no woman spoke to me to that effect—there is not the
least forgetfulness on my part—the prisoner was not led away by any of his mates, not on the 12th, the second night—he was not so much in liquor that he required assistance in going home—he had been drinking, but he knew well what he was about—he made four attacks on me and ran away—I had not taken him into custody that night before this—I was attacked in Pomeroy-street, about 150 yards from the Victoria, near the place where the prisoner lodges with a person named Gray—I knocked the knife out of his hand and ran after him, saying, "Mind the knife, he has stabbed me"—I ran 300 or 400 yards after him before I caught him—I fell with violence on the same shoulder on which I had received the injury—I ultimately took him into custody myself—I had never seen him before the night of the 11th—there was a man named Crutcher there—I cannot; say whether he had been drinking at the Victoria—he seemed to have been drinking, but he knew very well what he was about—the expression I referred to, about forty policemen taking him to the station, was used by the other man.
MR. METCALFE. Q. Was your shoulder cut before you fell? A. Yes; there were not any stones where I fell; but whether there were or not my shoulder was cut by the knife—the woman spoke to me after the prisoner had gone away the first time; I can't say whether she came to caution me—she did not say, "For God's sake, policeman, go away; whatever you do, don't interfere with them"—the prisoner came back to me while the woman was speaking to me.
CHARLES COX ( Policeman, P 346.) On 12th April, about twelve o'clock, I was in Clifton-fields—I met Luscombe in Pomeroy-street—I remember the people turning out from the Victoria public-house, and among others the prisoner—we were then standing on the other side of the street—the prisoner crossed over, pointed to Luscombe, and said, "That is the b—that took me to the station; it would take forty b—policemen to take me there to-night; if ever you put a hand upon me again it will be worse for you," or "I will do for you"—Luscombe told him that he had nothing to say to him, and said, u Why don't you go away home?"—a man then came out of the public-house with some beer; he asked for a drink—he said, "Why don't you drink and go home?"—the prisoner said, u The policeman did more than his duty last night;" and he said that he would keep in the street all night if he liked—I then went on my beat, and left Luscombe to go on his—a few minutes afterwards Crutcher called me—I went up the street, and found the prisoner in Luscombe's custody—Luscombe was staggering back, and said to me, "Look out for the knife"—I made a catch at the prisoner to catch hold of him very quickly, and I received a blow from him on the side of my head with something very hard; I believe it was not his fist—I did not see anything in his hand, but I think there must have been something beyond that which nature gave him—a stone in a handkerchief would produce such an effect as I felt; I was knocked down Benseless—two or three minutes after I came to myself I found that Lus-combe had taken the prisoner—I followed them to the station, and found Luscombe bleeding from the back—I saw the prisoner at the station, and found on him this stone ( produced)—it was done up in this handkerchief in the way it is now; there was a knot tied above the stone to keep it there—the prisoner was charged with stabbing Luscombe—he said, "You have not found the knife"—that was all he said—he had been drinking, but was not drunk; he knew what he was about.
Cross-examined. Q. How far is it from Pomeroy-street to the station; about a mile? A. No, not half a mile: it is about that—I have been
stationed there about eighteen months—I saw by the prisoner's manner that he had been drinking—I had not seen him that or the preceding day—I have known him by sight; I believe he works at England's factory—I searched him and found this stone in his pocket—I found Lusoombe staggering when I ran up—I did not see any one in the street but Luscombe and the prisoner—I saw no one after I left the public-house—I did not see Luscombe have anything outside the pnblio-house—I believe a pint of beer was brought out; I did not see Crutcher and Luscombe partake of it—I saw the prisoner take hold of it, but do not know whether he drank; I walked away; I took none of it—I do not know what Luscombe took—I lost my senses—I am quite sure of that—Crutcher took me up—I believe they ran into Kendall-street—I did not follow them.
JOHN HOLDAWAY ( Policeman, P 219). On Thursday night; in consequence of what Luscombe told me, I went into Pomeroy-street, and found this knife lying in the carriage-road, about two yards from the footway, near the spot where Luscombe described that he had been assaulted—it was open as it is now, and slightly wet with a little rain that had fallen—I took it to the station and gave it to the sergeant.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know the Victoria? A. Yes, it is about half way between the Victoria and the Queen's-road, about two hundred yards from the Victoria—I found it about 4 in the morning—I am sure that was about the time—while I was searching only one of the constables spoke to me, the one who was on the beat in Luscombe's place—no civilian spoke to me—I looked about the entrance to Kendall-street and Powis-place, which leads from Pomeroy-street to Kendall-street.
MR. METCALFE. Q. Did you find it near the place pointed out to you by Luscombe? A. Luscombe described that place.
ELIZABETH MATILDA COOMBES . I live at 45, Bedford-cottages, Peckham—on 11th April, in consequence of something which had been done to me, I gave the prisoner into custody, and went to the station—I had sent for a policeman—as I followed to the station, I heard the prisoner say to Luscombe, "I will do for you, I will watch you"—he used bad language, but I could not say what words—I never saw him to my knowledge before that night.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you drank with him on that night? A. By his pushing me into the place—he went quietly to the station after the policeman had hold of him—he was discharged there.
JOSIAH CRUTCHER . I am a dairyman in Pomeroy-street—on the night of 12th April, about a quarter to 12, I was at the Victoria public-house, and saw the prisoner—he was not drunk, but he had been drinking—he went out before me and was talking to the two constables—he was threatening Luscombe that he would do for him, for some grievance that had happened the night before—he said, "This is the b that took me last night, he shall never take me any more"—I got a pot of beer for my supper in a can and asked the prisoner to drink, saying, "Do not say any more, go home"—he drank, I believe, and Luscombe said, "I do not want to have anything to say to you, as far as last night, that is forgotten, and if you do not say anything to me I shall say nothing to you"—he walked on and I stopped behind—Luscombe followed him—I then followed and saw them some distance off struggling together—I stepped back and spoke to Cox—I afterwards saw Cox knocked down—I assisted him up and stopped with him—the prisoner ran away; Cox was insensible for five minutes.
Cross-examined. Q. How long had you been at the Victoria? A. About ten minutes—I had not been there before—I had left Deptford at 11 o'clock
—I do not say I had not had a glass of beer anywhere—I came home with an old gentleman; there were three of us, we had a pint or a pot, I don't know which—I was as sober as I am at this moment; I had half a glass of gin at the Victoria—if I had had two or three pints of beer it would not have made much difference to me—I heard the prisoner say, "No forty policemen shall take me to the station"—he was a little fresh —I did not know him till the day before, as I stood at my gate he walked up with two or three friends.
JOHN SWAN FLOWER . I am a surgeon, of Camberwell-green—I attended Luscombe, I found him in bed—he had a clean-cut wound about an inch or rather larger, penetrating to the bone—it had been given with considerable force, and much against the bone, and if it had not met with an interposition of that kind it would have been very serious—he was otherwise bruised on the shoulder, such a bruise might arise from a fall, but the other was a clean cut, and must in my judgment have been caused by some such instrument as this—he has been under my care ever since, and is still unable to use his arm.
Cross-examined. Q. How are you able to know that the bone was penetrated? A. It did not heal kindly, and an effusion of blood followed—I do not think falling on the shoulder immediately after or at the time would aggravate the appearances—if a man received a cut and fell on it the surrounding muscles might be affected—I do not think a flat stone would have done it—the macadamized roads with stones with projecting points could not do it.
MR. METCALFE. Q. Did you see the clothes? A. Yes, they were cat right through clean.
MR. LILLEY to J. CRUTCHER. Q. Did you see a person named Lowes after you had been before the police Magistrate? A. I never heard the name, and do not know any such person—I never spoke to a person on 25th April and said, "Good morning, Sir, you had your journey for nothing the other morning"—I do not know what you are alluding to.
MR. LILLEY called WILLIAM LOW. I am a blacksmith, and live at Pomeroy-street, Hatcham—I work in the same factory as the prisoner—he has worked there sinoe June last—he has borne the character of a quiet, inoffensive man—he lives in the same street, and I never saw him drink so much as half a pint of beer—I never knew him quarrel with a man—on 12th April, about twelve at night, I was going to the Victoria Arms, and saw him standing outside the door—he was very tipsy, I have not the slightest doubt about that—I said, " Charley, why do not you go home?"—he said, "Well, I am going," and made a bit of a move—the tallest policeman came across the road to him and said, "Are you going home?"— Charley said, "You mind your own business, you go and do your duty"—and he laid hold of him by the jacket and said, "It is my business to clear the street of such vagabonds as you"—he left go of him and he regained his feet across the road—the policeman took him by the neck and jerked him in that position from the Victoria down to Powis-court, where the prisoner got away from him—a man named Croucher, a cow-keeper, was there, but he was very tipsy, he could scarcely stand—I went to the Victoria for some beer for supper—I had not had as much as half a pint—I had been working overtime—when the prisoner got away from the policeman, the policeman drew his staff from one of his pockets, and Cox, the shorter policeman, hit him on the jaw and knocked him down—he ran to the other side, and the tall policeman hit him with his
staff across the middle of the arm, and then he ran across the road again and through Powis-court—I said to Cox, "Why do not you leave this man alone? he is a very quiet man, if you leave him for a few minutes he will go home"—he said, "Yes, I will, but my mate won't"—I saw no knife, if there had been one in the prisoner's hand at that time I must have seen it; I was close to him—I never saw him use a knife like this ( produced), and I have known him some months—I never knew him to have anything more than a small pen-knife, which he used to take pieces of dirt out of men's eyes with, and he used to borrow my knife sometimes—I used a rasp, and pieces of scale very often fly from the iron into the eyes—when I arrived at the Victoria Arms it was shut—(I saw Crutcher the same week that the hearing was at the Court)—the policeman was picked up by Crutcher, and he picked his hat up for him and wanted to know which way he had gone, which he actually did not know—I took the policemen down the street and walked with them—I saw no sparring between Luscombe and the prisoner with his staff—I saw him strike at him in the middle of the road—I could not hear any policeman say, "Look out for the knife, or mind the knife,"because I was behind the two policemen when Cox received the blow—I heard Luscombe make no complaint while I was there.
Cross-examined by MR. METCALFE. Q. You worked with the prisoner? A.
