No, I worked for the same firm—I was not out with him during the Easter holidays—I was at work—I went to the Victoria that night about five minutes past 12, for the first time—I had come from Greenwich, from my wife's mother's—I wfent to fetch my wife home—I went to the Victoria for my supper beer—I had a brother there, and I ran to get some beer—he is at home—I generally go to the Victoria for my supper beer, and they were just coming out—the house was closed, but I knocked at the door—the landlord opened it and I got the beer—the policeman was standing on the other side of the road—Luscombe came right across to the prisoner, and a number of men who had just come out of the Victoria—the milkman was standing on the other side of the way—I did not see the prisoner remain there after his mates had left—he was there—he remained by himself—there was nobody but I and the fmillrmjui—Luscombe came over and said, "Are not you going homef"—he said, "Go and mind your own business; go and do your duty," and Luscombe collared him by the neck, shoved him across the road, and said, "It is my duty to clear the streets of such vagabonds as you"—nothing was said about his having been previously at the station that night—he pushed him up the street, and Cox followed, and the milkman and I, until they came to Powis-court—I know where the prisoner lives—they went opposite his house, and pushed him past it—the prisoner got away when Luscombe was shaking him, and he put his hand behind to get his trunchepn—Cox ran in between, and the prisoner hit him with his left hand on the jaw—Cox lay on the ground about two minutes—Crutcher picked him up, and then he ran into Powis-court—that was immediately after Cox was knocked down—I swear there was no knife—it was just under a gas lamp, and if there had been a knife I must have seen it—I never heard a word about a knife—I do not know whether the policeman stabbed himself, but I never saw anybody by whom such a cut could have been inflicted on him—I never heard of it till next morning, and I am quite sure he was not stabbed that night; if he was it was when he was in the other street—I did not see him stagger while he was in my presence—I did not follow them to Powis-court or to the station—I went a week afterwards to the police-court—I did not know that the charge was not true, because he might
have done it in the other street—no one gave evidence in his favour that I know of—I have not visited the prisoner—I know him by working with him hut if a man comes in drunk we know that he is a drunken man—he works up to nearly 8 o'clock, and sometimes till 10—I am a blacksmith and boiler-maker—he does not work in the same shop—I never saw him drunk—if I go for a walk I might meet him by chance, just as I might meet you—I have no other means than that of knowing whether he is a sober man—I never saw him quarrel in my life—I never heard anything of his knocking down the woman on the previous night—I heard it at the police-court—I heard her say that he struck her, but never heard her say that she was covered with blood.
MR. LILLEY. Q. Did you ever see him drunk before that night? A. Never; I have had opportunities of seeing him before, of an evening—there were twenty or thirty men on the drink on this Easter holiday—they were not at work that week—I was at the police-court, but was not called—I went to know whether there was any truth in the case—I understood that the stabbing had been in the other street.
SAMUEL BARRACLOUGH . I am a boiler-maker, of Pomeroy-street, Hatcham—I work at the factory—I was with the prisoner on 11th April, in the evening, at a public-house in the Bath-road, and saw there the witness who has been examined here to-day, and another woman, Coombs—they were in the side bar of the public-house—the prisoner and I were drinking in the front bar—they beckoned to us to go to them—we did so, and they asked us for a drop of gin—the prisoner paid for half a pint of gin and peppermint, and a couple of biscuits—the prosecutrix and the female had some words, and went out—the prisoner followed her, and caught hold of her arm—she wrenched hold of his hand, said he had hurt her, and struck him—he took no notice, but took it in good part, and struck her again—she struck him on the face, in the jaw—she came inside, and he came after her—she struck at him again—he said, "Drop that"—she struck him again, and he said, "If you don't be quiet, I shall smack your face"—she struck him again, and he smacked her face—she was not covered with blood—shehad a slight scratch on her cheek, but I perceived no blood—we came out to go home, to get ready for work in the morning—they were outside, and said that they would give the prisoner in charge to the first policeman they came to—we went down Pomeroy-street, and met a policeman—she said, "I give that man in charge to you"—he said "What for?" she said, "For smacking my face" he took his lamp, looked at her face, and there was a scratch on it—the prisoner said, "She struck me several times, didn't she, Sam? " I said, "Yes"—he had his light there, and Baid, "Where did he strike you?" she said, "Here"—he could see no mark, but collared the prisoner by his handkerchief, and said, "You come with me," holding him with one hand, and the light with the other—the prisoner said, "Let me go; if you want me to go with you, I will go quietly"—the policeman let him go, and we all went away together to the station—I wanted to go in, but they would not allow me, and policeman 161 and the other went in together—I did not hear the prisoner utter any threat at that time, or strike Luscombe any blow—I was near enough to have seen him do so—I continued near them until they reached the station at Peckham—I am positively sure no blow was struck by the prisoner—I have known him since February—he has been quite mild since I have known him—he has not been accustomed to fight with people.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you not call knocking down a woman anything?
A. He did not knock her down, he simply slapped her face with his open hand—the mark on her face was not a bump, it was a scratch; his fingernail must have cut her face—I perceived no blood on her shawl; I can only speak to what I saw—he did not knock her down' m the public-house; he hit her with his open hand—he did not hit her with his fist on the jaw, and knock her down—I will swear she was not knocked down by him in my presence, and I was there all the time—I did not see her fall down—the other woman was sitting in a chair when this business was going on—the prisoner and I did not force her into the public-house, force her down into a chair, and keep her there—they did not try to leave the public-house—I did not hear him, when the policeman took him, Bay anything about two aach b—stinking w—s, nor "If you lay hands on me I will lay you dead in the road;" nothing of the kind—I followed them all the way to the station—he did not say, going to the station, "I will do for you; I will watch you"—I work with the prisoner—I have never drank with hint before that night, unless it might be a night when we were having a pint of ale after supper, and then gone home—I have never been in his company above twice before in a public-house—the landlord was not present at the Bath tavern when this little bit of a woman was beating this man—the mistress was there—I do not know whether she is here—I never saw the barman.
MR. LILLEY. Q. Did you see this man knock down Coombs or the other woman? A. No, and I was there all the time, so that I most have seen it—when the charge was made by the woman to the policeman, the prisoner mid, "She struck me several times over on the face"—the prisoner came away with me from the station.
SARAH CURTAIN . I am the wife of John Curtain, of Pomcroy-street, Hatcham—on the night of 12th April I was standing at my doof at half-past 11 o'clock, waiting for my husband—a policeman spoke to me as he passed; I have not seen him here to-day that I know of—I saw another policeman; a short one—they crossed over to the Victoria tavern, which is right opposite, and the short policeman stayed a little time, till the tail policeman came and went into the Victoria, and brought out a pint pot—those are the two policemen, ( Luscombe and Cox)—they partook of it; the tall one then took it back—Orutcher, the milkman, came up, and a joke passed between them—Crutcher was very tipsy—Mr. Greedy was turning them all out of his house, as it was 12 o'clock, and the prisoner came out and crossed to the kerb—I knew him before going backwards and forwards—they were on the edge of the pavement, against the sign-poet, drinking the beer, and Crutcher in front of them—I did not observe how the prisoner came out; it was very dark; they had douted the lamps—I went in half an hour before to ask for my husband, and the prisoner was very tipsy in front of the bar, bat I did not notice what state he came out in, whether steadily or not—my husband came oat after the prisoner, and persuaded him to go quietly up the street—I remained at my door the whole time till Vowles went away—my husband pursued him, but he said he would not go home, because he knew his mates were not gone home—when the policeman went away my husband took him up the street, and laid hold of his arm—Vowles needed support; he was very tipsy—when he was gone the policeman knocked at the public-house door—Mr. Greedy said, "Who is there?" and while they were talking, the prisoner came down again, and knocked at the door—the policeman said, "You go away home," in a very gruff manner—the prisoner said, "You go away, and go on your duty"—the
policeman said, "You had better be off home," and be pusbed him in jerks, and collared him by the back of the coat—he got him past where he lodged, and we did not go any further—I crossed the road, and saw him go away—he is a very rough, powerful man, but a very sober man—it is very seldom he gets drunk—the policeman said, u Do not be mistaken; we took him last night; he is the biggest coward out; I gave him two or three good shakings last night; he is the d—dest coward out"—he went up the street with my husband, and we heard a running away—the prisoner was very tipsy; he could scarcely stand—I did not see the policeman take his staff out; neither of them had their staves out in my presence.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you hear the prisoner say anything to the policeman? A. I did not—the policeman said something about going to the station last night, but the prisoner did not—the prisoner went across the road to the other side—the policemen were not there then—my husband was trying to get him home—he did not cross the road directly after he got out of the public-house—his mates live on the other side, and he said he would go and see if they had come home—I have seen one of his mates here, Barraclough.
COURT. Q. You saw him on this night? A. Yes—he was very drunk, and he went home before Vowles came out—the prisoner went and knocked at his door—the policeman was then on the Victoria side of the road—my husband went with the prisoner—I saw the prisoner talking to the policeman, but do not know what was said—there were no loud words at all, or noise, or hallooing, or disturbance—I am quite sure I saw no staff taken out—I saw the policeman with his staff out afterwards—we were still at the door when we heard the running, we had never gone in.
MR. METCALFE. Q. Is your husband here? A. No; nor is the landlord or landlady of the public-house that I am aware of—the policeman fell in Powis-place—he jumped up and ran again directly; and the prisoner stopped and the policeman stopped—the policeman stood on the pavement and the prisoner in the road—I said, "Oh! policeman, do let the poor man go home"—he went like this, and I said, "Oh, policeman, hit a woman!"—he said, "I will hit anybody that interrupts me"
COURT. Q. Where was Mr. Low? A. I do not know—he persuaded the prisoner to go home—I heard the prisoner's voice after the prosecutor said that—he cried for mercy, and he called for my husband; I knew his voice—Mr. Low was not there at the time my husband persuaded the prisoner to go away; he came after that, when the prisoner came back, but I did not know him—he was there when the prisoner said, "You go away home, and do your duty"—he went with the prisoner past my house, and I did not see what became of him after that—I did not see the prisoner fall—I saw Luscombe fall—I did not see Cox, the short policeman, at that time—I went up to where the policeman fell—I did not see Cox lying on the ground—my husband is at work; he has not beeu here—he did not know that he would be required—I did not go before the Magistrate—my husband was not very tipsy, but he had had enough beer.
ANN FOX . I am the wife of James Fox, of Pomeroy-street—on Friday night, 12th April, a few minutes before 12 o'clock, I sent my daughter Ellen into the Victoria, while I waited outside—I saw the two prisoners standing by the sign-post and one of them had a pot in his hand—I did not see the prisoner or hear his voice; all I heard was talking inside the Victoria.
ELLEN FOX . I am a daughter of the last witness—I went into the Victoria on 12th April, at a few minutes to 12 o'clock, while she waited outside—I
saw the prisoner then very much the worse for liquor—there were two persons standing outside, one had a pewter pot in his hand—they were still there when I came out, I saw no one with them.
ELIZABETH GRAY . I live in Pomeroy-street, Hatcham—the prisoner has lodged with me since a week before last Christmas—I knew nothing of him before further than seeing him pass to go to work—his character has been very good—he has been a sober industrious young man—I never saw this knife in his possession.
MR. METCALFE called ELIZABETH COMIS. On 11th April I had been in the Bath tavern with a young woman, a friend of mine, having gin and peppermint—the prisoner was at the private bar, but I did not see him at first—he had this young man with him ( Barracfough)—as I was coming out the prisoner was at the door with a glass of ale in his hand, and asked us to drink it; we refused—his friend caught hold of my friend and the prisoner caught hold of me by the arm, and pushed me back in at the private entrance, and asked us both what we would have to drink; we said that we had had quite enough—my friend said, "If we have anything we will have some gin and peppermint"—we had some and called for two biscuits—and then the young men went behind the door with my friend, talking to her—I was standing by myself, and wont out to see the time, I found it was twenty-five minutes to 10—I said, "Come, Sarah, it is time we got home, your mother will wonder where we are"—she came running out and the prisoner got hold of her in a shocking manner—I said, "You, leave her alone, and let her come on"—he instantly caught hold of me behind and got my head partly doubled to my feet—I said, "You good for nothing creature, you had better let me alone"—he said, "Come tick and have some drink"—I tried to get away, but could not, and he struck me on" the jaw and dug his nail into my hand; here is the mark now—he went away in at the private entrance, and I went in and stamped my foot and said that he should not strike me like that—I struck at him and he struck me two blows, one was over the eye, and I instantly fell senseless—the people ran round and got me in the parlour, ordered the company to go out, and begged me not to let the prisoner go away—the policeman brought Borne water, and my friend washed my face—I sent my friend out to see if there was a policeman—she never returned, and I found the two men walking away—I called, "Police," but no assistance came—I afterwards gave the prisoner in charge—the blows were struck violently with his full force; here is blocd on my shawl now, but it has dried in.
Cross-examined by MR. LILLEY. Q. Is Sarah here? A. No; I did not beckon to the men, or speak to them at all—nor did Sarah in iny presence—we drank with them—I do not get my living in any other way besides that which I have mentioned—I was coming home from my work in the Bath-road—I struck at the prisoner once, I cannot say whether I struck him—he did not show the policeman the mark on his face, he said, "She struck me on the face"—there was no mark to be seen—I mean to say that the blood on this shawl came from my face—my nose bled where the cut was, and over my eye-I mean to say that the blood trickled down from my nose—I have not washed it since—I had not passed half a dozen words with the prisoner, and I gave him no cause—I went with him to the station, and heard that I might take out a summons—I went to take one out, but did nob, because there was a misunderstanding; Mr. Mprton did not understand my woids.
JAMES LUSCOMBE ( re-examined). There was a good deal of blood on the front of the last witness's shawl—it came from the cut on her nose and eyebrow, and there was a little bit of a scar on her cheek.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you hold the light to her face? A. Yes; there was not the least bit of mark to be seen on the prisoner's face—the blood on the shawl was quite fresh—the charge was refused at the station—I showed the blood there, and the woman showed her nose.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY on the Second Count. — Three Years' Penal Servitude.
Before Robert Malcolm Kerry Esq.
[trialAccount t18600507-497]
497. ANN HOWLETT (24) , Stealing 1 watch, value 30 l., of Mary Patrick from her person.
MR. LLILLEY conducted the Prosecution.
MARY PATRICK . I am a widow, residing at 2, Frederick-terrace, Commercial-road, Peckham—on the evening of 5th April last, about half-past 5, I was coming out of my house—I had to go round the corner to Camden-grove, and as I turned, about twenty or thirty yards off, I saw the prisoner and another at the opposite side; they came up to me and asked me the way to the Kent road—I had to turn round to tell them—I pointed with my hand; they said a gentleman had directed them wrong the other day and asked me to show them right, and asked if they had to cross a field—I said, "Yes"—they then asked if they should be able to get an omnibus—I should say our conversation lasted about four minutest—during that time I had sufficient opportunity of seeing them, they were very near to me—the moment I turned round I missed my watch—they were then both in sight—I just looked down and saw that my chain was hanging—I turned round and said, "I have lost my watch, that woman has got it"—when I called out, the prisoner and her companion ran away; they were near enough to hear me—when I left my house I had my watch safe—I had not spoken to any one from the time I left my house till I saw these women—after I called out and saw the women running, a man came directly to me and said, "What have you lost?" and put his hands on each of my shoulders—I think it was to stop me, but I would not let him—I got away and followed after the prisoner and her companion as fast as I could—I never saw the man afterwards—I was at the corner that leads into the Commercial-road—I followed them and called out, "Stop the women, they have robbed me of my watch"—the prisoner was stopped almost directly afterwards, at the Rosemary Branch public-house—I got up at the time she was stopped—she had a muff in her hand; she was taken into the parlour and was kept there till Sergeant Castleman came—the other woman ran up a very quiet street, there was no one to stop her.
Cross-examined by MR. RIBTON. Q. Were there many people in the street? A. No, very few about—I can't say how many; it was not dark, it was about half-past 5—I had not met either of them before they came up to me—I don't recollect seeing any persons iu the street, these were the only persons I saw—I had looked at my watch the moment I came out of my house—the prisoner was taken before I got up, a few minutes' walk from where the robbery took place—a gentleman had hold of her when I came up—he is my next door neighbour—I don't think he is here—there was a crowd round the public-house—he came up in the crowd—I lost sight of her for a moment rouud Camden-road—when she was takeu I said, "You have got my watch,"—she said, "I am very
respectable, I have not" I—was very confident she had got it; I have never seen it since—they had got round the corner when I called out—they were near enough to hear me; they were about twelve feet from me, and about half as far from the comer—I lost sight of them momentarily—I saw them when I turned the corner—the other woman turned down a street on the right band side—I don't know what became of her—I suppose it is about two minutes walk from the corner to the Rosemary Branch—she declared she knew nothing about it.
MR. LILLEY. Q. Have you any doubt that this was one of the persons who enquired the way to the Kent-road? A. No doubt at all.
ALFRED CASILEMAN ( Police-Sergeant P, 38). In consequence of information I received on the afternoon of 5th April, I went to the Rosemary Branch—I there received charge of the prisoner—I told her the charge, and she said she was innocent—she said she lived at 34, Norland-square, Notting-hill—I went there—she said she had been to 34, Wellington-street, Peckham—there is no such street as that—I have been resident there between seven and eight years—I know every inch of the ground.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose the prisoner was searched? A. She was searched at Camberwell-station—I did not search her—she was taken in Camberwell—I have been in Peckham about eight years—there is not a Wellington-street in the whole of Peckham—I know all the new streets—there was not a great crowd, not more than half a dozen people—it is about 200 yards from where the old lady lives, to the Rosemary Branch. GUILTY .
The prisoner was further charged with having been before convicted.
WALTER HOLMES ( Policeman F 3). I produce a certificate—( Read: "Bow Street, August 25, 1856: Ann Waters, Convicted of stealing a gold watch. Confined Six Months.") The prisoner is the person who was convicted under that name.
GUILTY.†— Six Years' Penal Servitude.

498. THOMAS CHEESEMAN (20) , Robbery with violence on Dennis Bryan, and stealing from bis person 9 s. his monies.
MR. LILLEY conducted the Prosecution,
DENNIS BRYAN , the prosecutor, was called on his recognisance, but did not appear.
ANN JONES . I live at 17, William-street, New Kent-road—I know Dennis Bryan; he used to live at the same place; he did on Sunday, 29th April—on that day, about 2 o'clock in the afternoon, I saw him at the door of the house standing in the passage; some females came up; one of them tied a stone in her handkerchief and struck Bryan on the head—I saw the prisoner about ten minutes after—he rushed into the passage, knocked Bryan down, and kicked him violently while down he then got up and ran out, and the prisoner ran after him—I saw nothing more—Bryan left that house on the following Thursday; he had been to the police-court on Wednesday the day before—I was present when he was examined before the Magistrate—the prisoner was at that time charged.
Prisoner. Q. What time was it when Bryan was standing at his door? A. Just about 2 o'clock—I live down stairs; he told me he had been rubbed about half-an-hour after—he did not tell me he was coming round to your house with a policeman—the disturbance began by Harriet Cross coming round and challenging the other young woman—Bryan pushed her away from the door—they had not been drinking in our house—I did not see you take anything.
EMMA SHORE . I live 17, William-street, New Kent-road—on the afternoon
of Sunday, 29th April, I saw Bryan standing at the door of his house—I also saw the prisoner, he rushed into the passage, knocked Bryan down, and kicked him, and ill-used him very much—I saw him pick up two two shilling pieces, and he had more money in his hand—I did not see where the money came from, only from the floor as he picked them up—I saw that the prosecutor's trowsers were torn very much by the pocket—I had seen his trowsers before the scuffle; they were not torn at that time; no other portion of his dress was torn—Bryan then ran into the back yard, and the prisoner after him—the prisoner afterwards came up and went down the street—he walked out—Bryan was in the yard, frightened to come out.
COURT. Q. How old is Bryan? A. I don't know; he is not an old man—I saw some stones coming into the passage; there were some women throwing stones; I suppose at Bryan.
MR. LILLEY., Q. About how old is he? A. About thirty-two or thirtythree—he is rather thinnish.
MARY ANN M'CARTHY . I live at 3, Thames-street, near William-street, at the back of William-street—on Sunday afternoon, the 29th, between 2 and 3, I heard a disturbance there—I went to the door, 17, Williamstreet, and saw the prosecutor on the ground, and the prisoner kicking him—I saw two 2 s. pieces fall on the ground, and I saw the prisoner pick them up—I did not observe where they fell from—they were both struggling—I heard Emma Shore say it was Bryan's money—I saw the prisoner's left hand by the side of the prosecutor's pocket, and his trowsers were torn down—the money fell to the ground at the same time the trowsers were torn.
COURT. Q. Were you one of the women who had the stone in the pockethandkerchief? A. No; I saw the woman in the road getting a stone.
NOT GUILTY
Before Mr. Common Serjeant
[trialAccount t18600507-499]
499. WALTER GRIMSTONE BOND (32) , Unlawfully obtaining 3 l. from James Mitchell by false pretences.
MR. SLEIGH conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES PEEK . I reside at Blackheath—my son, Edward Peek, and another, carry on the business of bread and biscuit manufacturers at Mill-street, Dockhead—I occasionally go to the premises, though not a member of the firm—the prisoner was in the employment of the firm, and had the management of the stablea—there is no Mr. James Peek in the firm—I never authorized the prisoner to shoot, or in any way to dispose of, the horses of the establishment—there is no pretence for such an assertion—I cannot imagine why he should have said so—he never asked me anything about them, in the way of shooting or disposing of them.
Cross-examined by MR. LILLERY. Q. Although not a member of the firm, are you accustomed to go there frequently? A. Yes; nearly every day of the week when in town, on my way to town—I do not recollect going twice a day—I may have done so—I had no distinct office there of my own—there was a desk that I made use of, and of which I kept the key—it had papers in it connected with the business of the firm—I had some conversation with the prisoner on one occasion with respect to the horses—I think he was there about three months—I received information that the horses under his care looked rather better, and complimented him on it, by way of encouragement—he did not exactly complain, but he mentioned that two of them were not equal to their work—I think the work is rather heavy—
I do not know whether one of the horses was a grey aud the other a brown—I have been in the stables on one or two occasions when they were going to be repaired, but I think I never was there when the hones were there—I frequently write to a country traveller named Newman on behalf of the firm—sometimes I have given directions to persons in the establishment by direction of the firm, but only to the clerks; I do not remember any others—I was not in the habit of going to the factory at all—I do not think I have ever given directions without being asked—I do not remember giving any instructions except in this way, that when the clerks have been about forming a new set of books I have suggested several things, which they have carried out—so far as my being a friend and relation of the firm they had that authority—I do not know how many horses were under his care.
MR. SLEIGH. Q. Did you ever authorize him to dispose of those horses? A. Not at all.
JOSEPH BURGESS MEAD . I am general manager to the prosecutors—the prisoner was in their employment about three months, and had care of the stables and hones—I never authorized him to sell or shoot any of the horses—I asked him, on 30th March, if he had sold or offered for sale any horse or horses belonging to the firm—his reply was that he had done nothing of the sort; that he was aware such rumours were about, but they were entirely without foundation—every six months we take a general stock; this happened to be at the time of the general stock taking—it was the prisoner's duty to hand in, on 31st March, an account of the stock with respect to the horses—we did not receive it from him, in consequence of which I received this note from him—it was put in evidence at the police court—( Read: "Sir, I am so unwell that I cannot attend to my duties this day, but I hope to be able to do so to-morrow, and trust my absence may not cause you any inconvenience.— G. D. Bond")—information was given me which led me to go and see a milkman named Mitchell, who I found had possession of one of my master's hones—I did not see the prisoner from that time till after he was apprehended—I went to his house previously, and was unable to find him there—he was taken into custody by my directions either on the Tuesday night or the Wednesday morning—the two horses, which are the subject of this inquiry, had been, up to the 1st March, in my employer's stables, and had been daily used—I cannot possibly say when I saw them last—I saw two horses at the police court; they were my employer's property—I had seen them in my employer's possession within a week of the prisoner's apprehension—I saw them some time in March—one was a grey hone, and the other was a kind of a brown or bay.
Cross-examined. Q. The prisoner was what you call the general superintendent of the horses and the stables, was he not? A. Just so; he had also charge of the harness and things—I did not buy these horses—Mitchell may, at times, supply the firm with milk—he brings milk there, which is sent by railway, from the station—I was on the premises the whole of the Saturday, excepting when I was looking for some one—he was there in the course of Saturday, the 31st—my duties do not require my presence on the Sunday—he may have been there to look after his duties on the Sunday, without my knowing anything about it—on the Monday night I received the note which has been read—my time of leaving varies—on that Monday it would have been very late; I should think 11 or 12 o'clock, because we were engaged in stock affairs—on the Friday I was there till late, and the prisoner would have been there nearly the whole of the night—my impression
is that it would have been late in the evening when I left; on Monday—I have no positive recollection—I was in my office when I asked him about the horses—he came in to see me about some other matters, or was passing through the office—he would have been making preparation for taking his portion of stock in the factory—all the offices have windows looking into the yard, but mine is arranged so that I can see a little of both yards, but not so much of the second yard—Mitchell would generally drive into the one that I could not see so well—Mr. Peek's window looks directly into the second yard, and mine does partially—there were twelve horses on the establishment—the two that I saw at the police-court were lame—the work required of the horses in our employ is rather heavy; continuous work, and long journeys—it has been reported to me at times that the grey and brown horses were not in proper condition—then we had others, and they were relieved for the time—that might have meant that they were too lame to do their work without rest.
MR. SLEIGH. Q. Is your work very heavy, and requiring horses of peculiarly good condition to do it? A. Not very heavy—they have to go long distances—Mr. Edward Peek seldom or ever comes down there—Mr. Frean is the partner—he would be consulted in the business, but nothing would be done without my being made fully cognisant of it—if there had been any to be bought or sold it would have come under my superintendence.
COURT. Q. How many horses did you ever buy? A. There were two bought just as I came—the prisoner was never authorized by me or by any of the firm, as far as I know, to buy or sell any horses.
JAMES MITCHELL . I am a dairyman, of 6, George Yard, Berraondsey—I work for the prosecutors—early in March the prisoner called at my plaee and asked me if I knew any one who had got a horse to let on hire—I told him I did not, and that I wanted to buy one myself, a cheap horse—he said that he had two cripples of the firm for sale, and I could take either on trial—I first had a bay horse—I drove him to Waterloo station and back with milk to the premises, and then returned him, as he did not suit me—I went round a day or two after, and he sent me a grey horse, which he asked 10 l. for—I tried it, and gave him 9 l. 10 s. for it; 3 l. 10 s. first, and then 6 l.—I paid the 6 l. on 22d March; this ( produced) is the receipt for it—on 2d April he came to me, and told me he was disappointed in a horse coming in, and asked me if I could lend him the horse to work for the firm at 7 s. per day, and next morning he went to my stables for the horse—I sent to the firm for it next day, but did not get it back—the brown horse did not suit me, and the prisoner sold it in my presence to Mr. Coleman for 9 l. 10 s. and allowed me 1 l. for my trouble and for the introduction—after I had paid for the hone, and got possession of it, the prisoner came to my stable one morning, and said, "Do not say anything about having paid for the horse until quarter day, and then I shall have replaced the other horse."
Cross-examined. Q. You say you gave 9 l. 10 s. for the grey horse; was that a sufficient price? A. Yes, for a crippled horse—I refused the brown horse because he was a bigger one than the grey—he was crippled too—we had the chance of his getting better or worse; and if I went into a fair or any place, I should not like to give more than 10 l., in my judgment, for such a horse, I did not buy it on my own judgment, I asked other people's opinion about it: in consequence of which I paid the prisoner what I did—the prisoner offered a receipt—9 l. 10 s. was a sufficient price for the brown horse in the state lie then was—the prisoner told me I need not pay a
farthing for the grey horse, I could work it out, as I worked for the firm—I said that I would much rather pay for him—my man left my premises with the grey horse in my van before I concluded the purchase.
MR. SLEIGH. Q. Do you mean to persist in the statement that, in your opinion, the horse you bought was only worth 9 l. or 10 l.? A. I should not like to give any more for it; but I am not a horse keeper—I do not believe he was worth any more for any poor man to buy—I was examined twice before the Magistrate—it was on the second occasion that I told the Magistrate the prisoner said that I need not pay for him, but could work it out for the firm.
COURT. Q. You say that you took the brown horse back to the premises yourself? A. I returned him by my young man, who is not here—he took the brown horse back, as well as the grey, about 3 o'clock to the stable—I cannot tell whether that is a busy time, but I should say not—not many of the young men are going about then, they are out with their horses.
COURT. to J. K. MEAD. Q. Is 3 o'clock a time when you ars all about? A. We are all there at that time—it is quite possible that I may have seen the milkman coming in from the stable, and not known the horse in harness—we have a constant succession of carts and vans coming in at all times of the day—our men might know our horses if they saw them in the shafts of Mitchell's cart, but it is not likely—the stable man would, but he would not be there at that time of day—besides Messrs. Green and Peek's horses, others come in twenty times a day.
CLARA COLEMAN . I am a widow, and a greengrocer, of 23, Bedfordbury—on 23d March I bought a brown horse of the prisoner for 9 l. 10 s.—he said he was a cripple and unfit for the work he was doing.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you receive any information with respect to its value before you purchased it? A. No—I know nothing about horses, I have lately lost my husband, and my business cannot be carried on without a horse—the prisoner did not tell me that the horse was Peek, Frefen & Co's. but I heard that he was employed there.
MR. LILLEY. Q. Did not he say that the horse was not fit for the factory work? A. Yes—we were close to the Dookhead factory at that time.
JOHN TIMOTHY HUGHES ( Policeman, M 249). I took the prisoner on 3d April, and told him he was charged with stealing two horses belonging to his employers, Peek and Frean; he said "Mr. Peek said I might shoot the horse;" I said, "But there are two horses;" he said nothing to that—he did not say to me which Mr. Peek it was, but at the police court next day he said Mr. James Peek.
Cross-examined. Q. When he said that Mr. Peek told him to shoot the horse, was it not "to shoot or sell the horse"? A It is quite possible—I will not swear he did not—he did not ask me to send for Mr. Mead, the general manager—he did say that he was so satisfied that he could clear up everything he had done that he wished Mr. Mead to be sent for.
MR. SLEIGH. Q. Were you present next day when Mr. Mead was at the police-court? A. Yes; the prisoner had an opportunity of speaking to him—I saw another person, Mr. Welsh, the night before, to whom the prisoner said that Mr. Peek gave him permission to sell.
CLEMENT LOWER . I am a veterinary surgeon—I examined a brown and a grey horse, which I afterwards saw at the police-court—they were worth about 40 l. or 45 l.—they were not fit to be shot—they were both lame, but in pretty good condition, performing their ordinary work—they were fit for heavy work.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you assistant to Mr. Erne? A. Yes; he examined them and passed them for the firm—I knew that one of these horses was blistered and turned out some time before on account of the spraining of the tendon of the fore leg, that is lameness—they were both lame when I examined them—one was very lame—it is a lameness from which horses recover with difficulty—whether I would give 20 l. or 25 l. for a lame horse depends upon what I wanted it for—I personally examined the horses when they were passed.
MR. SLEIGH. Q. What was their value in your judgment at the time they were bought eighteen months ago? A. About 40 l. or 45 l. each—there are degrees of lameness—these horses were in a condition to work at the time—I saw them at the police-court, and they have been at work ever since—the lameness was a sprain—it is shod with leather now—that was done at the prisoner's wish, in consequence of the lameness.
MR. SLEIGH to J. B. MEAD. Q. Did the prisoner ever tell you that he had sold these horses, or pay any money on account of the sale? A. No; he denies having sold them.
GEORGE SMALL . I am a dairyman, of Hop-gardens, Charing Cross—I was present when Mrs. Coleman bought the horse of the prisoner for 9 l. 10 s —I paid the money and took it to Mitchell.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you pay the money at the prosecutor's stables? A. Yes; in the stable—Mitchell took the money oub of my hands—there was a stable man present, in and out, but he was not there at the moment the money was paid—we were all talking openly about the transaction.
MR. SLEIGH. Q. Was any one of the stable men joining in the conversation or standing with you three? A. No; nor within hearing that I know of.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18600507-500]
500. WALTER GRIMSTONE BOND was again indicted for unlawfully obtaining 3 l. of James Mitchell, and 8 l. 10 s. of Clara Coleman, by feloniously pretending that he had the authority of George Hender Frean and another to sell certain horses.
MR. SLEIGH conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES MITCHELL . The evidence given by this witness in the former case was read over to him, to which he assented, and added —When the prisoner offered me the horse, I believed it was one of the horses of the firm, and that he had authority to sell it; I paid the money under that belief.
COURT. Q. Are you the prosecutor of this indictment? A. No; if I do not get the horse I shall prosecute in the County Court.
Cross-examined by MR. LILLEY. Q. When you had got the horse, did you mention it to Simpson? A. Yes, and to all the men—I told them where I had bought the horse—there was no concealment in the transactions between me and the prisoner.
MR. SLEIGH. Q. Did you ever say one word about this before, that the firm knew you had bought the horse? A. Yes—I did not tell the Magistrate so when I was examined before him.
CLARA COLEMAN . The evidence of this witness was read over to her, to which she assented.
GEORGE MEAD . I am general manager to Messrs. Peek—Mr. Peek, junior, is under age, he seldom attends to the business—Mr. Frean's departmeut is attending the markets, purchasing wheat and flour, and arranging with me—everything that is done by the firm comes under my knowledge and cognizance—the prisoner generally receives his orders from me, but for
some little while till the business was more thoroughly established, Mr. Peek came down and assisted us—I never gave the defendant directions, directly or indirectly, to dispose of these two horses—( The witness's former evidence was here read over to him, to which he assented)—I received this notice on Monday morning, 23d April—he was there for an hour or two on Saturday afternoon—I went to his house the first thing on Tuesday morning, but he was not at home—I went twice that day and he was not at home—he did not come to the establishment, and I ordered him to be given in custody.
Cross-examined. Q. Supposing the prisoner wanted some clover for his horses, would not he ask Mr. Peek about it and not you? A. He might, because he obtained the clover from the Messrs. Peek, at Eastchot—that could be done without any communication with me, if he had arranged that the supply should come in that way—I do not remember that he has ever asked me, with respect to clover, during the whole time he has been there.
COURT. Q. Did he ever ask you for beans, or anything else? A. He would ask for oats aud beans because Mr. Peek bought them at the market—he never applied to me for cart grease—such things as breaking of harness were continually brought before me, before Mr. Bond came into the employ.
JAMES PEEK . The evidence of this witness was read over to him, to which he assented, and added —I was not there between the 20th and 30th March; I was in Devonshire—the last time I was there in March was about the 8th or 9th; I was there almost daily before that for one or two hours—it was about two months previously that I complimented him upon the look of the horses—I do not remember any conversation between us about the horses for a month previous to my departure for the country.
Cross-examined. Q. Does your memory enable you to speak with any confidence as to the last time you were there? A. I can remember up to the day I left town, that was the 8th or 9th,( Looking at a memorandum-book)—I made the memorandum at the time, it was on 9th March that I went to Devonshire, and it was about a month previous to that that I spoke to the prisoner about the condition of the horses, but I cannot say positively—I did not leave it to him to do what was right with regard to the horses—I did not order clover, straw, and corn for them—the prisoner gave me a list on two or three occasions, and I submitted it to Mr. Frean.
MR. SLEIGH. Q. Did you authorize the prisoner to do anything without your knowledge? A. Never.
EDWARD PEEK ( Junior), I am one of the firm of Peek, Frean, and Peek—the prisoner was in our employ—I never gave him authority to sell or shoot any of the horses, or deal with them in any shape or form—I never saw him before.
MR. LILLEY submitted there was no case to go to the Jury, as there was no proof that Mr. George Hender Frean had not given the prisoner authority to sell the horses. MR. SLEIGH contended, that having called one of the partners and the manager of the firm to prove that the prisoner had no authority to sell, that the case ought to go to the Jury, especially as it appeared, by Mr. Mead's evidence, that the primmer denied selling them at all.
The Court considered that in an indictment for false pretences in which the authority of two persons would be necessary, it was not sufficient to prove the authority of one, and therefore directed a verdict of
NOT GUILTY
[trialAccount t18600507-501]
501. JAMES BARRETT (19), and REUBEN STREAKER (19) , Feloniously forging and uttering an order for the payment of 153 l. 10 s. 3 d., with intent to defraud.
MR. BRAND conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN PEACOCK TURNER . I am cashier of the Unity Bank in Cannon-street—on 16th April this cheque for 153 l. 10 s. 3 d., which I have in my hand, was presented to me—the name of Talleman is at the foot of it—Mr. Talleman was a customer of ours—I cashed the cheque, believing this to be his signature, but I have since discovered it to be a forgery—it is very much like his signature—I cannot swear to the person who presented the cheque—Streaker is like the man—in cashing it, I gave a 100 l. note, 5 ten-pouud notes, and 3 l. 10 s. 3 d. in money—these are some of the notes that I gave.
COURT. Q. They are the same numbers you mean as those that were paid? A. Yes; I only swear to them by that.
HARRIS TALLEMAN . I am a general warehouseman, living in Houndsditch—I keep au account at the Unity Bank—this order for a cheque book is not in my writing—I never signed this cheque, but it is very much like my signatnre—I know the prisoner Barrett; he was in my employment for about five or six weeks, about five or six months ago—he had frequent opportunities of seeing my writing—he has transacted business for me at the Unity Bank; taken cheques there, and received money.
RALPH PAYNE . I am in the employ of Mr. Goad, jeweller, in the Borough—on 16th April I saw Barrett about 8 o'clock in the evening—he looked at two rings which would come to about 3 l. 15 s., and asked me if I could give him change for a 10 l. note—I told him I would see if he would put his name at the back—he put a name at the back of the note—I said I was afraid I should not be able to give him change for it—he said he was going as far as the Elephant and Castle, and would call again—this is the note—I read the name—it was "J. Williams, 18, City-road"—I know this note by the name—he went out of the shop, leaving the note—I kept it till he returned, and then told him that I was afraid I should not be able to change it for him—I asked him where he got it from—he said that he had a friend who came into 3,000 l.—I told him I was afraid I should not be able to change it for him, and he said he would go and ask his friend if he could lend him the money—he returned, and said that he was not able to find him—I told him if he left the note I should be able to change it for him in the morning—he said that he should be going past the Bank of England himself in the morning, and could get it cashed—he left me 10 s. as deposit on the rings, and weut away, taking the note with him—Mr. Lyons afterwards came and brought the note—he took it away again with him—I went and gave information to the police—I went to Mr. Lyons' shop that evening and saw Barrett there—there was a policeman there, who asked him if he had any more notes, and he denied having any more—I said that I had seen him with some more notes in a bag when he took the 10 l. note out—I saw a woman in Mr. Lyons' shop with him—he had not given me a 20 l. note that evening to change, nor had I given him a 10 l. note that evening.
CHARLES LYONS . I and my father keep a clothing establishment in the Borough—on the evening of 16th of April, Barrett came into our shop about a quarter to 9, accompanied by a young woman—he selected a stock of clothes, value 3 l. 3 s. and offered me this 10 l. note—he told me that he had just bought two rings of Mr. Goad, had paid him with a 20 l. note and had received this note in part change, and wished me to change it—I did not like his appearance—I told him that if that was the case, I would go over to Mr. Goad, and perhaps he would givemegold for it—I went to Mr. Goad's and saw Mr. Payne—I afterwards returned to my shop, and detained Barrett in the shop while Mr. Payne went for the police—he was eventually taken
into custody, and the girl also—Mr. Payne said in his presence that he saw him with other notes; but I saw nothing.
ROBERT PACKMAN ( City-Policeman). On the 17th, from some information I received, I went to Stone's-end police-station, and saw Barrett there—I told him I was sorry to see him there, and asked him if his friends were aware that he was there—he said he believed they were, but he had not seen them—I said I understood he was there about Borne notes—he said, "Yes—I am; I should like to tell you the truth about it"—I have known him some years—he knows me to be an officer—I then said, "You know I am a police officer, and whatever you say I shall have to make use of in evidence against you before the Magistrate"—he then said, "I received them notes from a person last night in front of the Mansion-house"—he told me that his grandmother had lately left him between 2000 l. and 3000 l. and that this was some of the money to go on with—he meaut the grandmother of the person from whom he had received them; he said, "He gave me these notes to get cashed for him"—I said, "Is that man a stranger to you?"—he said, "No; his name is Reuben William Streaker, and he lives somewhere near Kenuington-park"—I had, previous to seeing the prisoner, seen the four notes which the officer had—I said, "I see on the back of some of the notes there is the name of Sole and Turner, and they are solicitors in Aldermanbury, close to where you live"—he said, "Yes, I wrote the name of Sole and Turner on one of the notes, but not on all of them"—I said, "I saw you with a man in Finsbury-circus two or three days ago, who was a stranger to me"—he said, "That is the man I had the notes of"—I had seen him with another man as I told him—I saw the two prisoners together about three days previous to Barrett's being taken in custody.
Barrett. I wrote the name of Williams on the note. Witness. I did not have hem in my hand—I only saw them in another person's hand; I should not know them—I saw the name of Sole and Turner on one of them.
HENRY TIGHE ( Policeman, M 226). On 16th April, I took Barrett at the shop of Mr. Lyons, clothier, High-street, Borough—there was a female in his company—this 10 l. note was passed to me in Barrett's presence by Mr. Lyons—I asked Barrett where he got it from—he said he had been paid his wages in a 20 l. note, which he had chauged for two 10 l. notes—he said his employers were wine merchants—I asked him where the other 10 l. note was—he said that he had given one 10 l. note to a friend at the Elephant and Castle—I asked him if he had the bag containing the notes—he said he had not—I asked the female, in his presence—she said she had not the bag—not being satisfied with them I took them to the station—the male prisoner gave his address Barber's hall, Monkwell-street—Whiffin, the female, gave her's 54. Wood-street, Cheapside—she was searched by the female searcher, Mrs. Fenson, from whom I received this bag with the contents—it contained a 100 l. note, and two 10 l. notes—those are the notes which Mr. Turner has just seen—on the 17th, I called at 54, Wood-street, the address given by the female, and there I saw Streaker leaving the house—I then recollected having to seen him the previous night standing oustide Mr. Lyons'Shop during the time I was speaking to Barrett—I did not say anything to him on this occasion—I afterwards apprehended him—I told him that I wanted him for being coucerned with a man named Barrett, and a female named Whiffin, for stealing a quantity of notes—he said, "The notes are not stolen; I have done wrong, but I was led into it by Barrett"—I took him to the station, searched him, and found on him this piece of paper, which I now produce—the sums stated on this piece of paper amount to 153 l. 10 s. 3 d.
COURT to J. P. TURNER. Q. Have you any means of knowing what time of the day the note was presented? A. I know from my books that it was between 11 and 3 o'clock—I go out at 3 o'clock, and it was before I went out—I have the book with the list of the notes which I paid away—it is about the middle of the day's entries in the book—from that I suppose that it was about the middle of the day's business; probably about 1 or 2 o'clock—I was asked for a 100 l. note and five tens.
SARAH ANN FENSON . I am female searcher at Stone's-end police-station, Borough—I searched a woman named Whiffin, and found on her a 100 l. note in a bag, and two 10 l. notes crumpled up—I delivered them to Tighe.
Streaker's defence. The cheque was written by Barrett, and I presented it.
BARRETT — GUILTY .— Four Years' Penal Servitude.
STREAKER — NOT GUILTY .
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, JUNE 11 TH, 1860.
